DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
July 15, 1942
In Lima'a despatch no. 4138 of June 24,
1942, there is forwarded a memorandum contain-
ing an account of a visit by Mr. Emmerson and
a member of the Chinese Legation staff to
southern Peru, where they made a detailed sur-
vey of Japanese establishments and activities.
Everywhere they went they got into touch with
representatives of the Chinese community who
assisted them with information. The memorandum
contains detailed information regarding the
Japanese in each community visited. It was
found in some places, notably Arequipa, the
second city of Peru, that the Japanese com-
munity, though small, was powerful. In a
number of places the Japanese appear to enjoy
complete freedom to carry on business, hunt,
fish and travel. It is interesting to note
that in some places there were a considerable
number of Okinawans (Loochoo Islanders), who
appeared to stand aloof from other Japanese.
Chinese groups promised to keep the visi tors
from Lima informed of future activities of the
Japanese.

Lima, June 24, 1942.
MEMORANDUM
Mr. Woo and I left Lima by Panagra plane on Monday,
June 1, at 2:45 p.m., and arrived at Arequipa at 6 oclock.
Among the passengers on the plane were Dr. and Mrs. Robert
Burnett Hall. Dr. Hall of the University of Michigan has
been in Peru several months and is making a study of the
Japanese problem in South America. He is reporting to the
Office of the Coordinator of Information.
The Chinese Minister had written letters to the heads
of Chinese organizations in each of the cities we planned
to visit and had also provided us with letter of introduc-
tion to the Chinese Vice Consuls who, in each instance, were
Peruvian citizens. Everywhere we were received with en-
thusiastic cordiality. In fact, we believe that, aside from
the value of the information obtained with regard to the
Japanese colonies in southern Peru, the trip was extremely
worth while as a good will tour. The Chinese were pleased
to be visited by representatives of their own and the American
Government travelling together. The trip may even have made
them more conscious of their position as Chinese and of the
part they can play in the cause of the United Nations.
In Arequipa we met representative leaders of the
Chinese colony at the meeting hall of the Kuomintang on the
evening of Monday, June 1, and again at a hotel managed by
the President of the Chinese Society on the evening of
June 2. About 10 Chinese were present at the first meeting.
Using the questionnaire which had been prepared by the
Auxiliary Section and translated into Chinese (copy enclosed
with Embassy's despatch no. 3790 of May 23, 1942), Mr. Woo
interviewed the leaders of the colony and a general discussion
was held, in both Spanish and Cantonese. As stated in a
later section of the memorandum, the Chinese in Arequipa were
of the opinion that the Japanese colony there was small in
numbers but important because of the weelth and position of
its members.
During the day of June 1, we called on Dr. Alberto
Fuentes Llaguno, Vice Consul for China. Dr. Fuentes is a
leading citizen of Arequipa and, in addition to his private
law practice, is Vice President of the University, and Presi-
dent of the Writers and Artists Club. His wife is a niece
of President Prado. The American Vice Consul in Arequipa,
Mr. Dwyer, later accompanied us to call upon Mr. Blaisdeli,
the American manager of the Southern Railway of Peru, and
Mr. Mier y Teran, Chief of Investigation for Southern Peru.
We were not entirely convinned by Mr. Mier y Teran's assur-
anoes that there was no Japanese problem in Arequipa.
Dr. Hall was present at our meeting with the Chinese
on the evening of June 2. The leaders or the colony assured
us of their full cooperation and their intention to keep us
informed of the activities of the Japanese in Arequipa.
We boarded a Fawcett plane for Nasca at 10:30 on the
morning of June 3. The small eight-passenger plane makes
stops
ile

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
stops at four points in the course of a scheduled 2 hour
journey. Two of these landings, Atico and Chala, are made
on the beach as there are no airport facilities. The fog
hugs the coast at this time of the year and at one point
in the flight we found ourselves low above the water with
visibility xtremely poor. We arrived at Nasca shortly
after l oclock.
The Chinese Vice Consul in Nasca, Mr. Agustin Bocanegra,
is at the same time mayor of the town, publisher of the bi-
weekly newspaper, proprietor of a store and owner of a
cotton plantation. Aithough we had said we wished to in-
terview only a few of the leaders of the Chinese colony, more
than 25 Chinese gathered at the Kuomintang Hall to greet us.
The Vice Consul, Mr. Bocanegra, presided and toasts were
drunk to the success of the democratic cause. It was dis-
covered that the Japanese colony in Nasca is small and of
little importance. The night was spent at the guest house
of the gold mine, Sol de Oro, a few miles from the town of
Nasca. Two American families live at the mine, Mr. Meissner,
superintendent, and Mr. Graff, geologist. The mine is ex-
pected to be worked out and to close within a year. A site
for a new mine has already been selected and prepared. The
world situation has, of course, made uncertain the future
of gold mining.
On the morning of June 4, we left by automobile for
Ica, approximately 85 miles northwest. The road, of ccm-
paratively recent construction, is excellent. It passes
the various haciendas located in the slim, green valleys
of the tributaries of the Rio Grande. The principal ones
are El Ingenio and Palpa.
June 4 was a religious festival and the places of busi-
ness in Ica were closed. We interviewed the President of
the Chinese Society and he supplied us with a written re-
port on the activities of the Japanese colony, and a list
of the Japanese residents. The night was spent at the Gran
Hotel Salvatierra at the springs resort of Huacachina.
The trip to Pisco requires about two hours and is
made over a good road. A car was provided for our use by
the Chinese Society or Ica. In Pisco we interviewed re-
presentative members of the Chinese colony, the British
Vice Consul and the Vice Consul for China. Japanese ap-
parently enjoy complete freedom in Pisco and they travel,
hunt and fish without molestation.
The short drive from Pisco to Chincha was made in the
afternoon of June 5. At Chincha the members of the Chinese
colony extended us an especially warm welcome. The Vice
Consul for China, Mr. Luis Sanguinetti, although of Italian
descent, is thoroughly pro-democratic in his sentiments and
placed himself at our disposition during the entire time of
our visit. He has been closely associated with the Chinese
Colony, which numbers some 300 persons, for many years and
his father was Chinese Viee Consul before him. He is pro-
prietor of a noodle factory and bakery and is one of Chincha's
leading

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
eading citizens. He entertained us in his home, intro-
duced us to most of the businessmen in the town and the
following day took us in his own car to Cañete. Since our
visit he has come to Lima and. during a call on the Chinese
Minister presented him with a complete list of the Japanese
residents of Chincha, including ages, addresses and family
details, which has since been turned over to the Embassy.
Mr. Charles H. Dixon, a British resident of Chincha and
local manager of H. W. Beausire and Company, is well ac-
quainted with the officials in the community and states that
they are cooperative. He has been working with the Chief
of Police in the investigation of certain rumors concerning
Japanese activities. Mr. Sanguinetti told the Chinese
Minister that following our visit, he called upon Mr. Dixon
and offered his own cooperetion.
The Chinese Society provided transportetion to the
port of Tambo de Mora 7.4 miles away. There we interviewed
a Chinese storekeeper and obtained information regarding the
few Japanese who had had establishments in the port town.
The Chinese promised to supply any additional information
which came to his attention.
Mr. Sanguinetti and Mr. Li, president of the Anti-
Japanese Society of Chincha, accompanied us to San Vicente
de Caete on Saturday afternoon, June 6. The leaders of
the various Chinese societies met us at the Kuomintang Hall
and a discussion wes held on the subject of Japanese acti-
vities in the district. These Chinese were intelligent heads
of businesses in San Vicente and were genuinely interested
in giving us whet information they could. They insisted that
we should visit both the port Cerro Azul and Puerto Fiel, a
beach where the Japanese frequently assemble. Although we
had expected to return to Lima on Saturday, they were ex-
tremely solicitous that we make these trips and that we re-
main for a dinner they had prepared for Saturday evening.
The trip to Puerto Fiel is described in a later part of this
memorandum. Cerro Azul is 8 miles from San Vicente and
appears to be an infrequently used port. There were, how-
ever, numerous piled up bales of cotton awaiting shipment.
A Chinese was interviewed there who promised to cooperate
with the Chinese Society in San Vicente in the investigation
and reporting of Japanese activities.
The Japanese population in Cañete Valley is the largest
in any district visited and the Chinese here were found to
be more enthusiastic and more able to make investigations
than those in any other city. They promised to make periodic
reports to Lima and twice since our return have called on
the Chinese Legation to leave material which they have pre-
pared. One of the officers in the Chinese Society is a
photographer and has supplied numerous photographs of
Japanese citizens in the community.
We returned to Lima, Sunday afternoon, June 7, having
been absent exactly a week and having visited six communi-
ties.
It

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
It is true that many Chinese in Peru have been in-
different to political problems end have even continued
to trade with Japanese since the Sino-Japanese hostilities
began in 1937. However, the entry of the United States into
war with Japan was a vitelizing shock to Chinese patriotism.
The Chinese are proud that the United States is at last
fighting for and with them and they are more politically con-
scious than ever before. A young Chinese born in Peru told
me that until December ? Chinese nisei in Peru knew little
and cared less for their Chinese heritage. Now, he said,
they are proud to be Chinese and are intensely interested in
the progress of the war. I believe that trips of this sort
will utilize this force and will contribute materially to
the investigation and control of the Japanese problem in
Peru.
Incidentally, I wish to mention particularly the tire-
less and intelligent efforts of Mr. Woo of the Chinese
Legation. His excellent knowledge not only of Cantonese but
of Spanish, and his tact, personality and long experience in
Peru contributed much to the success of these investigations.

The population statistics tabulated for cach of the cities
visited have been taken from the Extracto Estadistico del
Perú and are the 1940 census figures. Unfortunately the
census does not separate Japanese and Chinese but merely
gives a total called "amarilla" or "yellow race". The
Chinese figures have been obtained from the Chinese
Legation and the differenoe between these and the total
"amarilla" census figure has been called Japanese. In
some instances these figures are contr-dictory to estimates
made by persons interviewed. At best they are an approxi-
mation and account must be taken of the fallibility of
census takers, the inconsistent counting of second-genera-
tion Chinese and Japanese and the changes which have
tial File
occurred since 1940.

AREQUIPA
The Chief of Investigation for Southern Peru stated that
there are 39 Japanese heads of families in Arequipa. Chinese
estimated the total number of Japanese at from 60 to 70.
The Japenese colony in Arequipa is smll but its members
are substantial business meln. On one street in the tom
were counted 7 Japanese establishments, principally bazaers
and general dry goods stores. These stores were well
stocked with merchandse and presented a prosperous appear-
ance. Funuya and Company, the largest Japanese importers in
Arequipa, are estimated to have made profits amounting to
s/500,000 1n 1941.
with the exception of two or three Okinawans, the
Japanese colony in Arequipa originates from Japan proper.
Some of the most successful business men came to Arequipe
from Madre de Dios department at the time the Peruvian
rubber market collapsed. Many Japanese had originally been
brought to Madre de Dios to work on the rubber plantations
there; wheu they became unemployed, some returned to Japen,
others migrated to other parts of Peru, and : few remained
in Puerto Maldonado and its vicinity where they raise tobacco
and mmnufacture panchos, boots, and other articles from
rubber.
There was unanimity of opinion among persons inter-
viewed in Arequipa--and these included the Manager of the
railroad, an American, the leaders of the Chinese colouy,
an American mining engineer, and the Chief of Iuvestigation
for Southern Poru--as to the most important and potextially
dangerous Japanese in the city. He is Yoshihisa Muto, an
architect, electrical engineer, and proprietor of dn
electrical supply store. Muto has lived in Peru more than
20 years; he has been in Arequipa 5 years. He was electrical
engineer with the Santo Domingo Mining Company. He is a
member of the Rotary and Jockey Clubs and is said to have a
wide circle of Peruvian friends. He is believed to be a
captain in the Japanese Army. He owns land in a Goverment
project called "Irigacion Silva". His office is in the same
buiiding as the British Cousulate. At one time Muto 1s said
to have approached the Chinese in Arequipe with e plan for
forming a joint Chinese-Japanese Society. He is believed to
possess detailed maps of Peru, especially the coastal re-
gion. He was seen frequently iu compeny with Jepanese from
the vessels which used to call at Mollondo.
Other propinent Japanese are as follows:
Luis Puzuxe. As stated above, Furuya and Company is
the largest Japanese importer in Arequipa. They sell mer-
chandise to Peruvian and Japanese stores.
Oktmake. Thie man is reported to be an officer in the
Japeanose Aray. His firm, a basear, has now been transferred
to

to a Peruvian pamed Fernendes. This transfer was effected
by Anibal Fepbandes, a lawyer and a member of the same

storo.
Sal1 Hermpos. This firm operates stores in Arequipe,

Shimisu. This Japanese owned a watch repair shop in
Arequiph which he has now transferred to a Peruvian. He

Airport.
The Japanese have not ma inta ined a school in Arequipa.
Their meetings are held in various stores and offices. At
present they us• Furuye's building.
In Pebruary, 194l, a Japanese vessel, the Aspita Maru,
was allegedly grounded near Mollaido, at a point 25 miles
north of the mouth of the River Tambo. The captain was well
acquainted with the coast and many people express doubts
that the ship actually ran aground. The vessel remaived
five months during which time it is saic that the officers
made detailed studies of the topography of the region. They
were seen iry Arequipa, often in the company of Y. Muto.
M. Mier y Teran, sub-inspector Jefe de Investigaciones
de la Region del Sur, stated in en interview that he had com-
plete information on every Axis National in Arequipe and the
southern district, all of which had been duly reported to
Lime. He showed us the list of German Mationals with police
reports on each but regretted that the Japenese list was at
the moment in the safe of his private home and not aveilable!
Mier y Teran is said to be close to the President and to
wield considerable influence in Peruvien official cireles.
He was deseribed by one person as a "go-getter" who had pull
with the Government because of his energetic investigation
of Apre activities. He expressed confidence that the
Japanese were effectively controlled but admitted that Muto
was a dangerous individual. Some person in Arequipe suggested
that Mier y Teran had not been immune to monetary persuasion
and that he was protecting Muto.x
The Japanese colony in Arequipa is a characteristically
solid, insulated group. However, it conteins no laborers
and no Japanese or Okinawans of the coolie class. Its mem-
bers are intelligent business men. For this reason, it
forms a particularly dangerous element in the community.

Nasca, one of the numerous valley towns of Peru, de-
pends upon water for life. Deficiency of water in the past
few years has seriously injured the cotton crop. The town
is awaiting the realisation of an irrigation project which
will bring renewed fertility to the valley.
There are no more than seven Japanese families in
Nasce. Or these, two are barbers, two operate combination
barber shops and basears, one is a mechanic, one a hotel
proprietor, and the other a restaurant keeper. Their names
follow:
Javier Toygsato - a barber.
Martin Tobosato, Hotel Central, Arica.
Pension/and restaurant.
Eduardo Inoguichi, Calle Limm.
Barber and besear.
Masay Subara - a mechenic.
José Asgto - restaurant.
Jos& Egbren and daughter, Josefina Egyren,
Calle Limm, barber y basaar.
The Japanese in Nasca have not been active. They have
long resided in the community and because of their small
numbers and relative inconsequence as individuals are not
believed to constitute an important danger.
In conversations with the Mayor of Masca, and with
Mr. Everett Graff, an American goologist at the mine, Sol
de Oro, and Mr. Guillermo de la Borde, one of the most pro-
minent haciende owners in the valley, the strategic import-
anee of Puerto Lomms and San Nicholas Bay was pointed out.
Both Germans and British submarines are said to heve used
Sen Wicholas Bay in World War I., and the terrain is waid
to be highly favorable to landing operat ions.
No Japanese are known to be living in Puerto Lomas.
Japanese vessele mlled at this port frequently before the
outbreak of war. Masay Suhara, the Japanese mechanie in
Nasce, is the only Japanese known to have visited these
ships while they were in port.

The Sub-prefect of Ica Province bas requested the
Chinese Colony to cooperate with him in the investigation
of Japanese activities. This is the first imstance of this
nature which has come to our attention. The President of
the Chinese Association stated that for three months, pur-
suant to the Sub-prefect's request, the Chinese bave been
making secret inquiries regarding the ectivities of
Japanese. Twice weekly they send emissaries to the various
haciondas surrounding Ica.
According to Chinese informants, there are approxi-
mately 35 Japanese heads of families in Ica and 20 nisei,
or Peruvian born Japanese. These are ell small merchants
and employees. There are no Japanese in the community of
substantial means. 70% of the Japenese residents are
Okinewans; there are seferal Koreans. The following are
the names of the Japapese businessmen in Ica. The most
important are G. Higashide, leader of the colony and Ysidro
Yshfdsu, proprietor or the largest store.

The Japanese operate a school which has an attendance
of some 30 pupils. The Japanese Association hes no fixed
place of meeting at the present time. Gatherings are held
in private homes and stores. As is true in all Japanese
communities, Tokyo radio is heard nightly.
The Japanese in Ice do not comment on the war and
there has been no noticeable activity among them since the
beginning of hostilities between Japan and the United
States. They have few relations with Peruvians or other
foreigners. The fact that the me jority are Okinawans of
little education and small financial means might suggest
that this colony is less dangerous than mny others in
Peru. Government authorities apparently exercise some sur-
veillance over the Japanese. However, there seem to be
pro-Axis elements among the population and there have been
unsubstantiated reports of pro-Axis activities both in Ica
and Huacachina, the springs resort a few miles out of town.
These are being further investigated.

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.98)

Chinese informants estimated the Japanese population
at 30 heads of families. The British Vice Consul placed
the total figure between 60 and 70.
The Japanese in Pisco are shopkeepers with the ex-
ception of two cotton farmers in the vicinity of towm who
work land of approximately 7 fanegadas each (1 fanegada =
7.4 acres). Seven Japanese business establishments are
found on one street, the Calle Marques de la Mancera. The
leader of the Japanese, who is the wealthiest and most
prominent business man, is Juan Yapamoto, who operates a
wholesale establishment on the Calle Independencia. He was
formerly President of the Japanese Association. Second in
importance is Juan Minemoto, proprietor of a watch repair
shop on Calle Comerci and now President of the Japanese
Association. There are two Japanese shops neer the port
of Piseo. Only two of the Japanese in Pisco are seid to
be Okinawans.
The Jepanese school is functioning with an enrollment
of some 30 pup1ls. Before December 7, 194l, the Japanese
held meetings in their school building. Since that time,
meetings have been held in various homes and stores. At
present the Japanese are said to meet in a Japanese
restaurant on Calle Marques de la Mancera. They assemble
nightly at ll oclock, presumably to listen to Radio Tokyo.
Japanese possess fishing licenses and fish on Sundays
and holidays without restriction or molestation. Further-
more, Japanese are known to own hunting guns and ammunition.
On April 30, the birthday of the Emperor of Japan, Japanese
left Pisco in seven automobiles and were absent for three
days. Four of them carried hunting guns and it is believed
they went to a nearby mcienda, having bribed the authorities
to obtain permission. On the same day ten other Japanese
came in to Pisco to celorate the Emperor's Birthday.
Japanese shops in Pisco appear to be well stocked with
merchandise and they are not believed to suffer particularly
from the restrictions of the Proclaimed List. They ere
said to buy particylarly from the Peruvian firms Papiso &
Company and E. Vargas and Company. There are also  number
of "Turkish" merchants in Pisco who deal with the Japanese.
Daring the months of September, October, and Movember,
1941, Japanese vessels called at the part or Pisco as often
as once a week. A Japanese named Tajate visited each one
of these ships. The British Vice Cohsul protested to the
authorities against these repeated and conspieuous visits
and Takate was not permitted to board the last two or three
ships which came to Pisco.
The Mayor of Pisco is believed to hold pro-Axis
sympathies,although the Sub-prefect of the province is
deseribed as favorable to the Umited Mationa. No special
preeautions appeer to be taken by the govermmental authorities
In surveillamee of Axis activities and the Japanese are free
and unrestricted in their movements.

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
One of the earliest Japanese settlements in Peru was
in Cañete Valley. The first Japanese school was established
in Santa Barbara in 1908. The Japanese population in
Cañete Valley 1s said to have reached 10,O00 some twenty
years ago. Most of these Japanese were employed in a
British sugar refinery. When this concern went bankrupt,
many of the Japanese returned to Japan and others moved to
various parts of Peru. The Chinese estimate the present
Japanese population in the valley to be 12o0. Towns with
the largest mumbers of Japanese are San Vicente, San Luis,
Imperial and Lunahuand. La Victoria is a Japanese hacienda.
The president of the Japanese Association of Cañete and
the owner of the haclenda La victoria was Hatsuso (Pedro)
Taghibene who was expelled by the Peruvian Government and
who departed on the SS Shawnee, June 15. Important Japanese
who will now direct the activities of the colony are T.
Mishima, Secretary of the Japanese Assoclation, and Kenso
Watanaba, Treasurer. Other Japanese cited as dangerous are
the following:
Sato. This man is a radio operator and is proprietor
of an cloctrical equipment store.
ada. A barber and Director of the Japanese Associa-
tions h Ts described as a fanatic.
Hayame. The proprietor of a general store whd is a
Director of the Japanese Associat ion.
Magda. This mn operates a store in the Hacienda
Unanue, Is said to be well educated and to be in cbarge of
Japanese propaganda.
Lato. A barber in Lunahuand who bought serap iron for
shipmodt to Japan and who invariably weut on board Japanese
vessels calling at the port (Cerro Asul).
Yguéni. In Imperial is said to be a Japanese army
ofricor.
Francisco Tukotaki. In San Vicente; a machine gumer.
Besides the farmers on the haciendas, the Japanese in
Cafete Valley carry on the usual occupations in mhich they
are engaged in other districts in Peru: storekeepers,
barbers, watch makers. Only l0% of the population is esti-
mated to be of Okinawan origin. Most of these are truck
farmers; mny of them live in San Luis. Chinese were of the
opinion that these Loochoo islanders were loyal Japanese,
although socially discriminated against by their cempatriots.
It wes remarked that Okimawans sat apart at Japanese meetings.
o

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
No collaboration between Japanese and other Axis
nationals has been noted in Cañete. One German, José
Esenwange, was cited as an ardent Nazi who at the same
time is a radio technician employed by the electric power
company. He is also a chemist.
The attitude of the general population in the Cañete
district was characterised as sympathetic toward the Axis.
Investigation and control of Japanese activities is said
not to extend beyond the requirement of permits for travel,
and these are easily obtaimmble.
The Japanese seem to have suffered little from black
list restrictions. They buy and sell merchandise without
difficulty.
The Chinese interviewed in Cafete were the most en-
thusiastic of any group met during the course of the trip.
They feel that the Japanese in the valley, because of
their numbers, location and influence, are a dangerous
•lement and they are ready to cooperate in the investiga-
tion of Japanese activities to the best of their ability.
They urged us to visit both the port of Cerro Asul and a
beach called Puerto Fiel where the Japanese frequently
hold meetings. Puerto Fiel is reached from San Vicente de
Cañete in about an hour and a halr by automobile over a
very bad road which passes near Hacienda Unanue and
Hacienda Santa Barbara, and through the towns of San Luis
and Imperial. As it nears the beach, the road becomes
mase wheel tracks in the sand. The beach 1s mmooth and is
protected by cliffs which run to the sea on both sides.
The water is said to be quite deep and to provide ample
accommodation for submarines. The Japanese hire trucks
and come in groups of 30 or 40 to this secluded spot.
Since our return, representatives of the Chinese
Society have mde two trips to Lime and have furnished
lists and photographs of prominent Japanese in Cañete
Valley. They have promised to supply further information.

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2-
The Japanese in Chinche are shopkeepers living in the
town. There are severel mechanics and one carpenter. The
leader of the Japanese colony in Chincha is K. Mupakata,
proprietor of two general stores. He is said to be a
captain in the Japanese army. He is recognised in the
district as the most influential of the Japanese. His
name was mentioned by persons interviewed in Pisco. Al-
though he formerly made frequent trips to Lime, the police
now refuse him permission.
There are ten or more Okinawans in the Japanese colony.
These attend Chinese moving pictures and have given money
to the Chinese cause. The Chinese in Chinche have asked
Peruvian friends to inquire why the Okinawans support
China. They replied: "We are not Japanese; we are
Okinawens."
Chincha residents think that 80% of the total popula-
tion is pro-democratic. There are no Germans in the com-
munity but a considerable number of Peruvians are of
Italian descent. One German mechanic was boycotted and left
town for this reason. The Sub-prefect is said to have re-
marked that there were still, however, certain pro-Axis
•lements among the Peruvian population of Chincha. As
nearly as could be ascertained, the authorities, provincial,
munieipel, and police, are pro-democratic in their sym-
pathies and are endeavoring to control Axis activities.
The police are at present investigating reports of a
clandestine radio transmitter operated by a Japanese on
a hacienda near Chinche.
Japanese invariably go to beaches to finm on Priday
and Sundey arternooms, when all stores in Chinche are
closed. There is a Japanese barber at the pert Tambo de
More. Many Japanese are said to come here at times when
their places of business are closed. There is some police
surveillemce. Japenese are not permitted to use beats
and the Chinese interviewed stated that Peruvian police
were on gward. There is e police station at Chimeke Baja
but not at Tambo de More. There are two Japamese farmers
in the Chinohe Baja district.

In reference to the Department's strictly con-
fidential instruction No. 1268 of May 18, 1942, en-
closing an instruction from the Chinese Minister of
Foreign Affairs to the Chinese Minister to Peru, and
in reference to this Embassy's acknowledgment in
despatch No. 3795/May 25, 1942, file No, 701, I
have the honor to suggest that the Department ex-
press to the Chinese Minister of Foreign Affairs
appreciation for the generous cooperation which the
SEP 18 1942
Chinese Legation is according this Embassy in pur-
suance of the instruction.
The Chinese Minister to Peru has continually made
good his offer to extend to the Embassy the facilities
of the Legation, and Mr. George Tsung-Yuan Woo of his
staff has devoted literally weeks Of his full time to
trips throughout Peru accompanying officers of this
Embassy and in other ways also has been most helpful.
Mr. Woo, whose father before him was in the Chinese
diplomatic service, was born in Peru, sohooled in the
United States, and has made a point of learning Cantonese.
His high inteiligence, diligence, and marked linguistic
talent have been of immeasurable service in this co-
PS/RIR
operative work, which has by consequence been very
suocessfully performed.
Respeotfully yours,

The follow ing is a paraphrase of a telegram of
August 18, 1942 received from the American Ambassador
at London:
I have learned from secret British sources that
certain Chilean newspapermen who have been visiting
Latin America will be employed by the Japanese Govern-
ment in Latin America. Moreover, Jorge Rese or Rosero,
a former employee of the Chilean Consulate at Yokohama
who is now to be transferred to Lima as Consul, is
reported to be carrying six war news films from Japan.
The Japanese are reported to be employing Rosero to colloet
American intelligence for the Japanese Embassy at Santiaga
and likewise to report movoments of Japanese in Peru.
Also, a person named Aburto is reported to be trans-
porting three industrial films. The Asame Maru which
arrived at Lourenco Marques on July 22 was carrying all
of these persons.

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.95)
THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
AMERICAN EMBASSY
No。 4618
Lima, August 18, 1942
Subject: The Japanese problem in Peru;
review and recommendation.
Fie Chuy
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
Fil
1942
5
大
FC g
894.20210/1
or Dis:t1
Divisionof
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
SE9-1942
DEPARTMENT OFSTATE
The Honorable,
Secretary of State,
Department of State
Washington.
Sir:
FILED
The present despatch is a review of the Japanese
problem in Peru as of August, 1942. It should be con-
sidered in the light of the detailed reports regarding
the Japanese situation in Peru which have been submitted
by the Embassy. Reference is made to despatch No. 4467,
dated August 5, 1942, File No. 820.02, and to the other
despatches mentioned therein. The conolusion arrived at
is that the most satisfactory solution would be the re-
moval to relocation points in the United States of all
persons in Peru of Japanese race. Such removal, if
feasible, is recommended by the Embassy as a long-range
objective and for immediate control and security, certain
other measures are urgently recomended.
A mass removal of Japanese from Peru is the one
permanent solution of the problem. It involves broad
questions

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
MAIL
-2-
questions of policy and serious difficulties; but for geo
graphic aud strategic reasons the Japanese problem on
the Pacific coast requires action that may be neither
possible nor necessary in the case of the Atlantic coast
areas of South America.
I. Numbers of Japanese in Peru.
The Embassy is of the opinion that there are approx-
imately 25,000 persons of Japanese race in Peru at
present (August 1942). The Peruvian census of 1940
(Extracto Estadistico del Perú 1940, published in 1942
by the Ministerio de Hacienda y Comercio) gives the
number of Japanese residing in Peru as 17,598, increased
by 8,290 persons of Japanese race born in Peru, or
a total of 25,888. Figures confidentially supplied
by the Ministerio de Relaciones Exteriores, Departa-
mento de Nacionalización, Extranjería y Inmigración
give a total of 26,728, not much different from the
above census total; but these confidentially supplied
figures give the number of Japanese born abroad as
considerably less (13,547) and of those of Peruvian
birth as considerably more (13,181) than the census.
It is known that during several years large scale
immigration of Japanese into Peru was carried on under
cover of a traffic in purchased Peruvian passports
representing the immigrants as Peruvians, and it would
appear possible that the discrepancy of figures here
noted is in considerable part a result of this traffic
and consequent efforts to conceal the misrepresenta-
tions involved. But the total is of more importance,
and here there is approximate agreement.
Since the time of the census in 1940 it is likely
that there has been a decrease in the number of Japanese
in Peru more than offsetting the natural increase by
births. Departures from Peru of Japanese returning
to Japan are known to have been heavy in 1941. In
the years just preceding 1940 a movement had already
set in which was carrying back to Japan a number of
persons of Japanese race, chiefly under twenty years of
age, considerably in excess of the contemporaneous
immigration of Japanese into Peru. In 1942 up to
August the number of Japanese in Peru has been reduced
by the approximately five hundred repatriated by way
of the United States.
The presence of large numbers of Japanese in Peru
is a matter of the twentieth century. There were Jap-
anese immigrants by 1900 but very few. By 1908 there
was a colony of Japanese in the coastal area of Peru
a hundred miles south of Lima sufficiently large to
warrant the establishing in that year at Cafete of the
first Japanese school in Peru. Growth of the Japanese
population in the Lima area was marked by the opening
there in 1920 of a Japanese sohool which has since ex-
panded

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.98)
MAIL
一3-
panded to very large size. Japanese immigration into
Peru was at its peak in the early 1920's.
工I。
Extent of Information Concerning
Japanese Activities in Peru.
With the approach and ocourrence of war the
Embassy has given close attention to the problem of
Axis activities, including Japanese. An Auxiliary
Section was set up in the Embassy for that purpose;
some of the efforts of the offices of the Military
and Naval Attachés were devoted to aspects of the
problem; the Commercial Attaché, particularly through
the operation of the Proclaimed List, cooperates in
this work; and an agent of the Federal Bureau of
Investigation has been assigned as Legal Attaché
to the Embassy to assist. All the usual contacts of
these offices have been made use of. The combined
information assembled by the Embassy through these
various offices is great. Good cooperation exists
among them, and there will be maintained a single
filing and index system of that material which is of
common interest to them all. This will include one
single master file of names of individual enemy aliens
and suspected agents.
In the obtaining of information about the Japanese
the Embassy has profited by the cooperation of Chinese
sources in Peru. Even before the Embassy communicated
to the Chinese Minister the instruction from the
Chinese Minister of Foreign Affairs which was contained
in the Department's strictly confidential instruction
1268 of May 18, 1942, the Chinese Legation had shown
Itself fully responsive to the Embassy's suggestions.
The method whioh the Embassy has found most productive
has been aotually to visit and interview leading
Chinese throughout the country, accompanied by an official
of the Chinese Legation. Following out this procedure
Secretaries Coville and Emmerson have together visited
all the points of principal interest, as regards
Japanese activities, throughout the coastal region of
Peru north of Lima, as well as the oentral area in
the Andes; and Mr. Emmerson, before Mr. Coville's
arrival, similarly traveled through the coastal region
south of Lima. No such visits have so far been made
to the trans-Andean parts of Peru in the Amazon Basin,
although two informants in this region are preparing
reports for the Embassy. The Consulate at Iquitos
also is reporting to the Embassy regarding the Japanese
situation in that district. There are Japanese who
reside in the vicinity of Iquitos and reports indicate
that a Japanese communication system may operate between
Peru and Brazil via the Iquitos area. This matter is
being investigated further.
One fruit of the cooperation of the Chinese is
that the Embassy now possesses, or shortly will possess,
a fairly complete name-by-name list of the Japanese
1n

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
一4-
in Peru outside the Lima area itself. For the
Lima area the Embassy possesses a photostat of a list
containing the names of some six thousand Japanese,
which list was originally compiled by the Japanese
association in connection with a voluntary offer by
the association to collect on behalf of the Peruvian
authorities the tax required for the registration
of aliens. But the number of Japanese in the Lima
area far exceeds six thousand.. The 1940 census, in
giving the distribution throughout the country of
the total of 17,598 Japanese residents of Peru,
places 13,557 (77%) in Lima and 1,996 (11%) in Callao.
Presumably the Peruvian-born population of Japanese
race is likewise concentrated principally in that
area. The work involved in the Lima area naturally
is much greater than for the remainder of Peru.
Visits to localities throughout Peru have
proven that assurances given to tho Embassy by the
Peruvian Government with regard to restrictions on
Japanese are not being enforced by the local officials.
In spite of assurances given us in Lima, (1) Japanese
in fact move about with little or no hindrance;
(2) many of them continue to reside at places from
which they are said to have been removed; (3) in
spite of regulations to the contrary, they are in
some places permitted to hunt, fish and drive automo-
biles; (4) most of the twenty-seven Japanese schools
in Peru (which in 1940 had a total enrolment reported
to be 4,256) are operating, but under restricted
conditions; and (5) most of the Japanese associations
still exist, although also operating under restricted
conditions.
III. Removal of some Japanese Already Effected.
After the United States was attacked by the
Axis powers in December, 1941, the Peruvian Government
agreed to a policy of removal of dangerous enemy aliens.
In April and June, 1942, three ships (the Etolin, Acadia,
and Shawnee) carried full lists of enemy aliens to the
United States from various of the American Republics
including Peru. Some five hundred Japanese went from
Peru on these three ships.
It was the effort of the Embassy to induce the
Peruvian Government to send on suoh ships those Japanese
whom the Embassy (on the basis of the mass informa-
tion above desoribed) considered as being the most
dangerous. This effort was only in part successful.
Great pressure and influence were brought to bear by
various persons upon the Peruvian offioials for the
exemption of many of the Japanese named by the Embassy,
and bribery no doubt played a part. Meantime the
Spanish Embassy, as representing Japanese interests,
had supplied the Peruvian Government long lists of
Japanese applicants for passage on the ships, and
the

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.99)
MAIL
5
shemuvian r ies rplases my names on our
list by mames of volunteurs listed by the Spanish
Embessy. Furtlernore, the Peruvian Government waited
until the last moment before informing us of the list
of aliens to leave upon each sbip, thus seriously
hindering additional suggestions on our part. The
Minister of Government proved in several respects un-
cooperative. Upon the return from the United States
of President Prado there was great improvement in
Peruvian cooperation.
Unless the Peruvian Government commits itself in
advance to accept the Embassy's recommendations, further
visits of ships like the Etolin, Acadia, and Shawnee
for the purpose of removing selected lists of enemy
aliens considered by us dangerous will unquestionably
be confronted by the same complication. On the other
hand, President Prado, in view of his expressed opinions
on the Japanese problem, might be persuaded to use his
personal influence to achieve successful action. If
not, the devious channels of influence in this country
wouid operate in the direction of striking from our lists
many names, replacing them by names of other aliens who
desire to leave Peru. There are large numbers who
desire to leave. Many Japanese wish by this means to
undertake to get back to Japan whenever it may be pos-
sible. Earning a livelihood in Peru has become diffi-
cult for them. Many Japanese shopkeepers carried
largely Japanese merchandise, now impossible to obtain;
the operation of the Proclaimed List has increased
their difficulties; they sense a growing suspicious-
ness and hostility directed toward them; they are appre-
hensive of worsening future conditions.
The Peruvian officials have consistently indi-
cated a readiness to expel any enemy alien against
whom a subversive record is proven. The Embassy has of
course been unwilling to make such evidence the sole
criterion. Our selection of Japanese to propose
for removal from the country has generally and neces-
sarily been on the basis of importance in leadership
of the Japanese community. Teachers of Japanese
schools, heads of local Japanese associations, former
Army or Navy officers, leading businessmen--such indi-
viduals, we have held, are potentially dangerous re-
gardiess of police record.
The Embassy is continuing to assemble information
looking to the discovery of enemy aliens who should
be removed from Peru and is at present prepared to
submit a list of at least two hundred Japanese heads
of families who, it is known, are leaders in their
respective communities. In spite of the difficulties
in oonnection with repatriating additional numbers of
Japanese, there would be decided advantages in remov-
ing even a few hundred of the Japanese leaders who
are considered most dangerous. President Prado might
agree

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.99)
一6
agree to such procedure as a first step in the
possible removal from Peru of the bulk of the
Japanese now resident in the country. Certainly
the psychological effect of such a step upon the
Japanese here would be extremely salutary.
IV. Permanent Considerations.
The permanently best solution of problems raised
by the presence in Peru of persons of Japanese race
requires the considering of further points. We must
weigh possibilities and probabilities both while the
war continues and in the period following the war.
Our experience is such as to prove that Japanese
emigrants always remain unabsorbed, homogeneous
communities directing their loyalty to Japan and ready
always to perform such services as Japan may call
upon them for. Such is the present attitude of the
Japanese in Peru and such, no doubt, it would continue
after the war.
Peru's location on the Pacific coast of South
America must make her always a potential source of
danger to the United States by reason of the possi-
bility of Japanese attack or even landings with the
cooperation of the Japanese residents. We are confronted
by a problem too large for Peru to handle herself,
and, in view of the probable importance of military
actions in the Pacific, too threatening for us to
ignore. This is true during the present war, and must
remain so after the war if the Japanese continue to
reside here. In the Embassy's opinion (and conversa-
tions which I have had with President Prado lead me
to believe that he is of the same view) the best
permanent solution of the danger would be to remove
all persons of Japanese race in Peru to relocation
points in the United States, there to await the same
disposition at the conclusion of the war which may be
found for similar residents of the United States who
are understood to be already assembled in relocation
centers.
Already the economy of Peru is adjusting itself,
apparently without difficulty, to constantly decreasing
economic activity by the Japanese, and it does not
appear likely that the exodus of the Japanese would
have any adverse effect which the country could not
overcome. It should also be recalled that the Japanese
have not intermarried much with non-Japanese Peruvians,
and consequently a mass removal would not be con-
fronted by the popular opposition which would be ex-
pected if family ties with Peruvians were the rule.
The Embassy advances a further reason in favor
of removal of the Japanese from Peru. It appears a
matter of certainty that the post-war course of events
in the American Republics as well as in many other
parts of the world must involve marked economic-social
changes in the direction of betterment of the condi-
tions

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.99)
XAIL
-7-
tions of the people at large. These adjustments
in Peru will be difficult. They will stand a far
better prospect of satisfactory outcome if they are
not complicated by the existence of what is now
Peru's largest alien group, industrious and thrifty
but with a low standard of living, separatist,
unassimilable, always disliked, non-democratic, and
permanently loyal to an anti-democratic state.
V. Immediate Considerations.
I believe that President Prado would like to find
a permanent solution of the Japanese problem during
his term of office. He would like to rid the country
of its large Japanese colony under some plan whereby
there would be no danger of the return to Peru of
Japanese after the war. Some of the President's
able legal advisors have pointed out, however, that
a serious question under international law would arise
if Peru were to send its Japanese residents to relo-
cation centers in the United States. Peru is not at
war with Japan and would be sending Japanese nationals
to a country which is at war with Japan. Immediate
action on the definitive solution of the Japanese
problem in Peru can not well go beyond continued
study and exchange of views with President Prado,
and an estimate of the possibility of finding sufficient
ships to transport some 25,000 persons.
Concentration of Peru's entire Japanese popula-
tion probably would not be practicable, although the
Spanish Ambassador, allegedly in order to facilitate
the representation of Japanese interests, would like
to accomplish this. It might be dangerous to concentrate
the Japanese in any larger groups than exist at present.
Furthermore, experience has shown that the venality
of Peruvian officials, poor organization, and lack
of effective control over the morements of Axis nationals
within the country probably would result in the failure
of any plan to concentrate large numbers of Japanese
in a few centers.
The Japanese problem is, however, of immediate
importance. Up to the present the Japanese have been
grimly quiet. We know that they hold the firm convic-
tion that their Emperor's forces will finally triumph,
although it must be noted that the bombing of Tokyo
was a blow to their morale. We know that they are
prepared to do their part when called upon by Japan
and that their organization is thorough centralized,
and efficient. It is the Embassy's opinion that
neither ertensive sabotage nor armed uprising is
likely unless ordered from without and synchronized
with an attaok from without. Should Japan's war
strategy diotate an attack on the west coast of South
America, then the Japanese here, in their present
status, would constitute an active danger of the grav-
est oharacter. Furthermore, the acute and ever
inoreasing

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
increasing distress caused by economic restrictions
and the application of the Government's recently
enacted expropriation decrees will not improve the
mood of Peru's Japanese. For immediate control and
security and until a permanent solution can be effect-
ed, it is recommended that we seek from the Peruvian
Government the following:
1. An agreement in principle to expel Japanese,
whether or not they are naturalized Peruvian
citizens. A number of the most prominent
leaders of the Japanese colony have escaped de
portation because of the fact that they are tech-
nically Peruvian citizens.
2. An unconditional agreement that the known
leaders of the Japanese colony now remaining in
Peru will be expelled on the first ship to be
sent to Peru. President Prado's expressed
interest in the problem should make this possible.
One ship then could be sent immediately to evacuate
these persons.
3. The removal of all Japanese from areas desig-
nated as strategic and from an area within a
specified distance from the coast. Japanese have
been evacuated from Chimbote, Casma and a few
other port cities, but often to points only a
few miles inland. Many still remain in the
coastal cities. Evacuation of Japanese from
Callao would present certain problems because
of their position in the foodstuffs business,
but satisfactory solutions could be found.
4. The establishment of relocation centers in
the interior for small groups of Japanese removed
from specified areas.
5. The closing, unconditionally, of every Japanese
school.
6. Assignment of special police to districts
where Japanese are living. They should be well
paid and should be transferred frequently, in
order to reduce the temptations of bribery.
7. A ban on possession by Japanese of short-
wave radio receiving sets.
8. Finally, the use of radio broadcasts (from
local stations,or from Amerioan stations, should
the ban on short-wave receivers not be imposed),
similar to those now direoted to Japan from the
United States, to counteract the propaganda
which Japanese in Peru hear from Tokyo and to
raise doubts in their minds about the ultimate
success of Japan's Great Asia War. While no
propaganda is going to change Japanese loyalties,
the

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.96)
-9-
the general population, if their leaders are
removed, might be convinced of the futility of
overt acts on their part.
Respectfully yours,
R. Henry Norweb.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF THE AMERICAN REPUBLICS
September 1, 1942
Summary of Lima Despatch No. 4618,
August 18, 194:, Concerning the Japanese
Colony in Peru; Review and Recommendation
The Embassy believes there are approximately 25,000
persons of Japanese race in Peru at present; 77% living
around Lima and 1l% around Callao. Although about 500
Japanese have been repatrieted in 1942, the Embassy stil1
believes the problem of Japanese activities quite out of
control. It reports that Japanese move about with little or
no hindrance; that many of them continue to live in places
from which they are said to have been removed; in spite of
regulations to the contrary, they continue to fish, hunt, and
drive automobiles; most of the 27 Japanese schools in Peru
are operating; most of the Japanese associations still exist,
although operating under restrictive conditions.
The Peruvian Government has taken some steps to control
Japanese activity, of which the deportation program was one.
This program, however, was "only in part successful". If
this country sends to Peru more deportation ships, President
Prado "in view of his expressed opinions on the Japanese
problem might be persuaded to use his personal influence to
achieve successful action. If not, the devious channels of
influence in this country (Peru) would operate in the direction
of striking from our lists many names, replacing them by
names of other aliens who desire to leave Peru". The Embassy
at present has a list of at least 2o0 Japanese leaders whom
it desires repatriated.
The Embassy states that it believes. "a mass removal of
Japanese from Peru is the only permanent solution of the
problem". However, such a program is at present impractical
because Peru is not at war with Japan, and because of the
difficulty in finding sufficient ships to transport some
25,000 people. Therefore, the Embassy makes the following
recommendations concerning the Japanese program:
1. An agreement in principle to expel Japanese,
whether or not they are naturalized Peruvian citizens.
2. An

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
2. An unconditional agreement that the known
leaders of the Japanese colony now in Peru be expelled
on the first ship sent to Peru.
3. The removal of all Japanese from areas
designated as strategic, and from an area within a
specified distance from the coast. (Although some
Japanese have been removed from the coast now, they
are still within a few miles from the shore. In other
strategic points no evacuation has taken place at all).
4. The establishment of re-location centers in
the interior for small groups of Japanese removed from
specified areas.
5. The closing, unconditionally, of every Japanese
school.
6. Assignment of special well-paid police to
districts where Japanese ere living.
7. A ban on possession by Jananese of short-wave
radio receiving stations.
8. The prosecution of a propaganda campaign to
combat propaganda from Tokyo.
ETL
RA:ETL:ES

CONFIDENTIAL
The Department of State transmits to the following
the attached information in the hope that it will be of
value:
Military Intolligonoe Division
Office of Naval Intelligence
Federal Bureau of Investigation
Enolosure:
Copy of strictly ponfidential despateh no. 4618
dated August 18, 1942 from the Amerioan Embassy,

From: J. Edgar Hoover - Director, Federal Bureau of Investigation
Subject: Chilean journalists returning from Japan.
A confidential reliable source has advised that Chilean
journalists who have been in Japan are expected to proceed to the United
States, and that the Japanese at the Japanese Embassy in Santiago
appeared to be very disappointed that these journalists were not going
to Buenos Aires first.
Among the returning journalists is an individual named Iglesias
and his wife who are reported to be very anti-American and probably
Japanese agents. This information has not been confirmed by investigation.
This information is also being furnished the Coordinator of
Inter-American Affairs.

Department of State.
Subject:
Japanese Problem in Peru.
Reference is made to your letter of transmittal, o
894.20210/198 of September 9, to which was attached despatch No. 4618,
dated August 18, from the American Embassy in Lima, Peru.
894.20210/200
' On page 8 of reference despatch the American Ambassador
has made specific recommendations for the handling of the Japanese
problem in Peru. The Navy Department considers it to the national
interest that these recommendations be placed into effect. However,
since it is improbable that all of them can be immediately implemented,
the following are regarded as meriting priority:
1. The removal of all Japanese from areas designated
as strategic and from an area within a specified
distance from the coast.
2. A ban on the possession by Japanese of short-wave
radio sets.
3. The closing, moonditionally, of every Japanese school
MAY 1 3 1944
FILED
The assignment of special police to districts where
Japanese are living. They should be well paid and
should be tranaferred frequently in order to reduce
the temptations of bribery.

I have the honor to enolose a translation from
Japanese of a communication addressed to José Dodobara,
enclosed in an envelope addressed to Roberto Nakashima
PS/RIR
N., Casilla postal 1790, Lima, postmarked Trujillo
and signed "Don José". The addressee, Jos6 Dodohara,
has been manager in Lima of the prominent Japanese
firm, Julio Tao. He is among the leaders of the
Japanese colony and was recommended for expulsion on
the S/s SHewnes by the Embessy. Roberto (Shoj1)
Nakashima is the fishing expert who directs an er-

心
Although
1942

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.98)
MAIL
-2-
Although the identity of the writer of the letter
is unknown, the subject matter and the style of his
writing suggest that he is educated and that he is
an important member of the Trujillo Japanese colony.
Reference is made in the communication to an
investigation of Japanese in Trujillo which the writer
attributes to the "manipulation of the Lima Embassy
of our enemy country, the United States". This
investigation is undoubtedly one being conducted
for the Ministry of Government and for the President
by Major César Larco. It is an official undertak-
ing and includes the taking of affidavits from indi-
vidual Japanese with regard to their antecedents,
family, business activities and financial resources.
An examination is being made of the accounts of
Japanese firms and general information collected
with regard to the movements and activities of
Japanese in the Trujillo area. A Japanese cook
employed by Mr. Larco and trusted by him, accompanies
him and acts as interpreter and is apparently the
Japanese mentioned in the communication.
As in numerous other letters which have been
translated by the Embassy, the patriotism and supreme
confidence of the Japanese in Peru clearly stand out.
The British and American fleets are called impotent,
the European situation described as "smashing triumph
for the Axis Powers", and the raid on Dieppe termed
a "colossal failure".
The inference is made that the United States,
by bringing on the war, has caused economic suffering
and distress in the countries of South America,
especially Peru. Apprehension is expressed lest
a "grave social crisis" develop. Countries who
have broken relations with the Axis or declared war
are called weak in their "blind subservience to
England and America".
Respectfully yours,
 Monvnt
R. Henry Norweb

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 1 to despatoh no.
4850, September 10, 1942, from
the Embassy, Lima.
Translation from Japanese into English of a
letter addressed to Sr. Roberto Nakashima N.,
Casilla postal 1790, Lima, postmarked Trujilio,
for José Dodohara, signed Don José.
It has been some time since I have had news of
you. This year we have had extremely cold weather
but I believe the cold has already passed its
peak, since now it is quite warm here in the middle
of the day. How is it with you and how is your
family?
Actually I have wanted to get in touch with you
for some time but since the beginning of the Great
East Asia War there have been so many difficulties
of all sorts and then I know that in the Lima Post
Office there is an especially strict censorship
of all mail matter for Axis people. As you probably
know, Matsuura has arrived here with a suspicious
official and, apparently for the purpose of finding
out some weak points among the Japanese population,
is closely questioning the Japanese daily. If this
were being made on the request or on the order of
the Peruvian Government, then it would be something
that couldn't be helped but it is being conducted
through the manipulation of the Lima Embassy of our
onemy country, the United States. The whole thing
is certainly an unconscionable business and while
I dont suppose we are going to receive any punishment
as a result of this, it is a disgrace that one of
our own people shouid have turned out to be such
a dishonest rasoal.
As you are aware, the Great East Asia War is
making tremendous progress. The restoration of the
occupied territories in the south is progressing
moothly and we are able to get all kinds and
plenty of natural resouroes. In Japan proper,
the orops for the entire country are the best this
year since 1933. Therefore, the inorease in war
time food production is a triumphal success.
With regard to the military situation, our armed
forees have brought under their control ali of the
former Amorican bases for attacking Japen in the
Northern, Central and Southern areas. This means
that the continued existence of our fatherlamd, the
Great Empire of Japan, is an immutable certainty.
In Europe the war situation is resulting in a mmeshing
trimph for the Axis powers. From the pelitieal
standpoint mglend is faced with the power of an
this questien is a matter of the sravest import which
may decide the fate of the Great British Mmpire.

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
The retreat of the Soviets means the retreat of the
Allies. All of the important newspapers in both
London and New York have agreed that this is true.
Churchill went to Moscow because of Stalin's in-
sistence on the immediate opening of a second front
and disregarding the opposition of his military
advisors, ordered carried out the raid on the little
port of Dieppe in northern France on the 19th of
last month. This raid turned out to be a colossal
failure and was the laughing stock of the entire
world.
Looking at Japan's offensive, about 40 warships
and 50 airplanes transporting numerous troops and many
kinds of war materials to northern Australia, which
the Allies must make their central base, were dis-
covered by the Imperial Navy and now repose on the
deep bottom of the Pacific Ocean. According to the
Berlin Radio, every day ten warships are destroyed;
this reveals the impotence of the two main fleets
of Britain and the United States.
America dreamed that she could bring Japan to
surrender without shedding blood and by a victory
won from an economic blockade but, on the contrary,
now she is the one who is being blockaded. For
this reason even the trade of the South American
countries has come to a standstill. Especially is this
true of Peru and for this reason the Government here
has suffered a severe economic blow. Commodities
are daily becoming soarcer and prices are bounding
up to a limitless ceiling. There is strong feeling
and anxiety that a very grave social crisis will
break out. We feel that the political leaders of
this oountry must wake up immediately to the situa-
tion and devise some thorough means for meeting it.
Aocording to the news from Tokyo this morning,
Foreign Minister Togo has resigned his office because
of personal reasons. His post will be filled by
General Tojo who now holds conourrently the port-
folios of Prime Minister, War Minister and Foreign
Minister. It is expected that the Imperial foreign
polioy will take a new direotion. The enemy countries
are especially worried about this. As Prime Mimister
Tojo has said on various occasions, England and the
United States are Japan's enemies. Certain countries
submitting to the pressure of Englakd and the United
States have broken relations or have deelared war
on the Axis powers. Now, they dont know which way
to turm and the fact that they repemt or their
blind subservience to England and America shows what
weak countries they are.
Well I have rambled on quite a while and close
sending my best regards to your family.
Dom Josd

The Embassy has in a previous despatch estimated

Mr. Shin-ichi Yokoyama, an Attaché of the Japanese
Legation in Lima, is the author of an article entitled
"Activities of the Japanese in Greater South Amerioa"
(Dai Nan-bei to Hon-po-jin no Katsudo) which appears in
the same publication of the Japanese Central Society in
Lima from which the above figures are taken. He sketches
briefly the commercial and industrial enterprises of the
Japanese in the countries of South America, with special
reference to Brazil and Peru. He emphasizes the strength
of the Japanese in Brazil, pointing out that the value
of their agricultural production in the state of Sao Paulo
alone amounted in the year 1936 to ¥ 150,000,000. He
concludes that the principal stage for Japanese activities
must be Brazil and Peru because of the great superiority
in numbers of Japanese residents of these two countries.
Mr. Yokoyama's final paragraphs reiterate the
spirit which has animated the Japanese in South America
and hint significantly at their ultimate aims. They
are quoted in translation:
"At present Japan is proceeding with
significant steps in her advance on the continent
of East Asia. This mission we must carry out
regardless of saorifice in order to secure and
extend the vital life-line of our Empire. For
this reason it is possible that expansion toward
1
South

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
South America cannot be considered for the
moment; however, for the very reason that we are
engaged in the "China Incident" we must foster
and develop to the fullest degree agriculture
and mining in this South American area.
Japanese activities in the South American
countries have so far been in the hands of the
first generation and we must await the time when
the responsibility will be shifted to the Japanese
of the second and third generations. Now the
responsibility rests squarely on the shoulders
of every Japanese here.
Will the day not soon come when the children
of the Japanese immigrants who landed on the
east coast of the South American continent
cross the Andes and shake the hands of their
brothers of the west.coast in the-upper reaches
of the Amazon!?"
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:

1-1608
Mr. Reid called on Mr. Hornbeck and, as Mr. Hornbeok was
busy, was referred to me.
Mr. Reid said that he called at the suggestion of Mr. Carl
Crow.
Mr. Reid said that for the past few months he has been
1n South America for the Foreign Poliey Assooiation working
on the Jubject or Japanese activities in that area. He said
that he had collaborated with our missions and partieularly
9
with Mr. Armour and that he had made all his material avail-
able to our missions or to the appropriate military ofrieials.
FILED
5
Mr. Reid said that Mr. Armour has collected material,
some of it with Mr. Reid's help, on smuggling or tungsten ous
direeted by the Japanese. He did not know whether means had
been found for shipping the tungsten to Japan or whether the
P
activity was designed merely to deny the tungsten to the
United States. He mentioned that a Nr. Franeis Herron had
disoovered these activities while in the Argentine on behalf
of the Institute of Current World Affairs. Mr. Merron, meeore.
ing to Mr. Reid, has om-returned to the United States to be O
drafted. Nr. Reid expressed some disappeintment that Nr. Herres
could not remain in the Argentine to continue his imvestigatione.
Mr. Reid

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Mr. Reid said that the mines in question are owned by a
Mr. Williams who was aware of losses by smuggling, but had been
unable to ascertain the methcds employed until recently, when
he apparently received assistance from Mr. Herron and Mr. Reid.
Mr. Reid also mentioned that in 1937 hehad known at
Harvard a Japanese Lt. Commander named Ko Nagasawa. In Lima
Mr. Reid found Nagasawa running a restaurant. Our military
attaohé told Mr. Reid that in addition he knows of a Japanese
major who is running a laundry in Peru.
Mr. Reid also said that there is sizable and well or-
ganized distribution by the Japanese of anti-U. S. propaganda
in the Argentine. He remarked that this propaganda was notice-
ably not anti-British. Aooprding to Mr. Reid, Japanese pro-
paganda is distributed by one Pugndlin, a former Argentine
minister to Japan who served under Irigoyen. Pugnalin has an
office in a German-owned building and receives nine thousand
pescs a year from the Mitsubichi Company. Mr. Reid believes
that in all probability he is also supplied with free living
quarters. Pugnalin is able to get a great deal of material
into the local press and he also personally distributes to
friends an anti-Japanese pamphlet which is so drafted as to
evade the restriotione of Argentine law.
Mr. Reid said that another Japanese agent in the Argentine
1s an Eurasian born in Malaya and thus a British subjest, who
was formerly the General Manager of the N.Y.I. Line in the
Argentine. This man, whose name Mr. Reid did not remember,
1s married to a Chinese, and is believed to receive and dis-
tribute Japanese funds to Japanese agents in the Argentine.
He has been partioularly active in following shipping, and has
an elaborate network of agents located throughout South Ameriea
who report to him. He himselr makes frequent trips up and
down the coast to communioate with these agents. Mr. Reid
says that all visiting Japanese are received by him before they
call on the Japanese minister. The British Embassy at Buenos
Aires says that so far sufriciently definite proor is not
available to warrant taking action against this man.
Nr. Reid said further that in Chile a Swiss-German named
Bye 1is the liason between loeal Nazi greups and the Japanese
1n Chile. Mr. Bye 1s the manager or Thomas Cook and Son.
Among Mr. Bye's activities, according to Mr. Reid, are attempts
to buy up in the interest of Japan small mines, apparently
for the purpose of keeping them out of produotion. Mr. Reid
said that Mr. Bye has succeeded in obtaining two small copper
m1nos,

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
mines, and two small cobalt mines whioh were both bought in
English names.
Finally Mr. Reid said that he had received information
that large numbem of Japanese are filtering into Paraguay
from Brazil. From Paraguay they drift into the neighboring
mining regions, especially the tungsten regions of Bolivia,
Argentina and Chile. Mr. Reid said that he had been informed
by a former President of Paraguay that in February of this year
some 700 Japanese reached Paraguay from Brazil. According to
Mr. Reid the Paraguayan Government does nothing to prevent this
because it desperately needs additional Japanese farmers and
hopes that many of those that come from Brazil will remain in
Paraguay.

SUMMARY OF JAPANLSE MSSAGES
The 34 sheets inoluded on this roll of film constitute 15 separate
otters dated between Sept mber 20 and october 2, 1942. Al1 these letters
are related, and although parts of some are no: lugible from the film, all
appear to be leritimato businoss and personal messages and with the possible
exeption of the letter last mentioned below, do not appear to indicate any
aoversive activities. A b ief summary of heir contents follows:

These letters do not bear the name of the addressee, and are
signed only by illegible signs perhaps intended as initials. They concern
chiefly negotiations by members of the busi ess from which the letters are
sent, with the Argentino Foreign Minister and Minister of the Interior to
secure for the Japanese firm in question the ight to operate freely, and
without restriotions s to communications, traveling, eto. These negotiations,
however, appear to be still in the formative stage, and little that is conorete
ie said about them. Business matters also are discussed, as dealings in wool
and saltpeter, insuranoe, and custody of money. In letter SA - 9 the writer
states that his new telephone number, 31-025, is not listed in the telephone
book. In letter SA - 8 are mentioned the namss, Senora Varendo and Antofagasta,
and Mr. Cuadola (phonetio spelling), representative of the Saltpeter Co. Also
the writer says in this lett r that Mr. Iahii is disappointed because the
lotters he sent to Lourenoo Marques hed been sent back to Argentina. (The
"SA" proceding the numbers of these lotters possibly stands for the initial

From: J. Edgar Hoover - Director, Federal Bureau of Investigation
Subject:
Japanese Radio Group
As of possible interest to you there is being tranamitted
herewith information which was received from a reliable, confidential
source concerning a radio intercept sent by the Japanese Ambassador
894.20210/212
in Argentina to the Foreign Minister at Tokyo on Ootober 9, 1942.
668
This intercept stated that at the conference of the anti-
20210
American Standing Defense Committee at Montevideo on September 1,
it was decided that citizens of the Axis countries should be
subject to strict regulation with respect to entering and leaving
Brasil and Argentina. It was stated in this intercept also that
the countries of Brazil and Uruguay completed successfully negotia-
tions concerning an agreement on boundary defense. This agreement
was signed by General Vargari, Argentine's Chief of General Staff,
and by the Brazilian Army and Navy officers who recently met in Rio
to discuss joint boundary defense arrangements.

SUBJECT: JAPAN TOURIST BUREAU-ORGANIZATION
3
