For Drstpin
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
894.20210
The Honorable
hy toss with.
The Secretary of State,
331-43
Washington
MAR 3D1943
Sir:
I have the honor to enclose translations of docu-
ments handed over to the Embassy by a confidential source,
giving a picture of the entire Japan Tourist Bureau or-
2
ganization for the Japanese fiscal year l94l (April 1,
1941 to March 31, 1942). These papers include (1) a des-
patch in April 194l, from former Manager T. Tsutsumi (now
in Japan) to the head office in Tokyo giving interesting
plans and suggestions for future work by the Bureau in
South America; (2) the 194l budget for expenditures by
PS
Japan Tourist Bureau Offices throughout the world; and
(3) an explanation by the head office of this budget and
/TL
the manner in which the budget items are to be used by
the various offices.
The main impression gained from a reading of these
documents is that Japan Tourist Bureaus are much more
concerned with propaganda work than with travel. Propa-
ganda appropriations in 194l accounted for Yen 843,000
(US$200,000) or over one-half of the total appropriation
of Yen 1,560,000 (US$370,000). Printed propaganda matter,
motion pictures and advertising accounted for Yen 454,000
or over one-half of the propaganda appropriation.
Japan Tourist Bureaus operate almost entirely on fund
received from Japanese Government sources. Of the Yen
FILED
1,560,000 receipts for 1941, Yen 400,000 was a government
151943
subsidy, and Yen 850,000 was received from the Railway
Ministry.
Appropriations for expenditures for l94l include Yen
116,Ooo for operation of the head office in Tokyo, includ-
ing an East Asia Tourist Conference in Manila in November
1941. Yen 425,000 was appropriated for the operation of
ten overseas offices in New York, Buenos Aires, Berlin,
Rome

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Rome, Peking, Tientsin, Shanghai, Hongkong, Manila,
and Hanoi. The Berlin and Hanoi offices were newly
opened, and the London and Paris offices closed. Paris
personnel was transferred to New York. Personnel was
low paid, but the manager and also generally secretary
of each office was given a liberal "travel expense" item
and "reserve fund" upon which they could draw for neces-
sary expenses.
The propaganda expense item as already mentioned
totaled Yen 843,000, a decrease of Yen 203,000 from the
1940 appropriation. The decrease was accounted for main-
ly by a falling off in ropaganda directed towards the
United States, including invitations extended to Americans
to visit Japan at the expense of the Japanese Government.
The main propaganda prop was Yen 225,Oo0 for printed pro-
paganda material, to cover a diversity of folders, book-
lets, pamphlets, etc. to be issued in a number of lang-
uages. It also planned collaboration with the German
Propaganda Ministry in undertaking a magazine for Germans
called "Introduction to Japan and Manchukuo". The motion
picture appropriation of Yen 165,000 was a Yen 30,000 in-
crease over l940, but even so provided for only four pro-
paganda films sound tracked in a number of languages. It
is interesting to note that imported film was to be used
for duplicates for European distribution, while Japanese
film was to go to "backward countries". This reference
to backward countries, apparently including South America
caused Buenos Aires Manager Tsutsumi to protest that "you
should not regard this as a backward country, for whether
rightly or wrongly it thinks of itself as being far super-
ior to Japan in culture, living, etc." In connection with
films it is also notable that Japan had no color film
equipment in l94l but planned to obtain Agfa technicians
to set up color facilities.
Appropriations for newspaper and magazine advertising
were reduced from Yen 160,000 in 1940 to Yen 73,000 in
l94l, perhaps because of an anticipated refusal of pro-
Allied newspapers and magazines to accept Japanese adver-
tising. Local Manager Tsutsumi tells of the ultimate
failure in l940 of his office and the then Japanese Lega-
tion in Buenos Aires to get La Nación to print a 26o0th
Japanese Anniversary Number, the newspaper's change of
heart he suspected "was due to the interference of a third
country." Manager Tsutsumi wished that he might make use
of the extensive and effective circulation network of the
British-American group of newspapers in Argentina. He
recognized that political propaganda would hardly do, but
hoped that acceptance of cultural and sightseeing propa-
ganda, and pictures in the weekly rotogravure sections
would prove to be a possible method of using the Allied
press.
It is noteworthy that the Tourist Bureau appropriations
provided only Yen 10,000 in both 1940 and 1941 for radio
broadcasting propaganda, as radio was apparently felt to
be

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
be a poor propaganda medium for the work of the Bureau.
Apparently part time propagandists had not proven to
be effective, for of the small staff of 15 attached to
various offices five were to be dismissed, and Manager
Tsutsumi protested against the appropriation for an addi-
tional part time propagandist to be added to his staff,
preferring instead to open a branch in Rio de Janeiro.
On the other hand the item for Delegated Propaganda was
increased to Yen 94,000 from the figure of Yen 53,000 in
1940. It would seem that this fund covers part of the
clandestine activities of the Japan Tourist Bureaus, es-
pecially the item of Yen 80,O0o for "commissioned business"
expenses being paid directly by the head office "for form's
sake", and including the travel expenses of agents, enter-
tainment, and hiring of employees for special work. Japan-
ese Chambers of Commerce (or Trade Mediation Boards) ap-
parently did some delegated propaganda work on behalf of
the Bureaus, but Manager Tsutsumi was not satisfied with
delegated work.
Under the item for Invitations (to visit Japan) extended
by the Bureau to those foreigners thought to be most use-
ful in spreading Japanese propaganda, the Bureau planned
to extend invitations to about 80 persons including Chinese
and Japanese in the Far East, and requested the Bureau of-
fices to recommend others from their districts. The Buenos
Aires Manager wished to have literary lights, newsmen and
artists invited so that he might obtain their collaboration.
He also suggested the importance of inviting promising
nisei (second generation Japanese born in Argentina) students
to foster their love for Japan. He also wished to have films
sent to Argentina to show to the nisei through local Jap-
anese Associations. The New York office was excepted from
extending invitations to visit Japan. The appropriation
for Yen 96,000 for Planning Investigations in 1941, com-
pared with Yen 13,000 for 1940, included a large new item
of Yen 84,Oo0 for investigation of ways and means of ex-
panding Japanese propaganda. It might also have been another
source of funds for clandestine activities on the part of
the Bureaus.
While the Buenos Aires branch of Japan Toupist Bureau
Office is still open at Florida 746, it is understood to
be in charge of the former secretary, Mr. Ehara. Needless
to say there has been no travel work for it to do sinoe
Japan entered the war. It carries some window exhibits,
and occasionally sponsors a flower show or photographic
or art exhibition, one being planned for May l943, for
which it is advertising in the Japanese press for exhibits.
However, it is not impossible that the organization of agents
in other South American countries continues to operate under
the ultimate direction of the Japanese Government. An ef-
fort will be made to obtain a list of agents of the Bureau.
Mr. Masao TSuDA, Domei representative in Buenos Aires
also has his office in the Tourist Bureau, and it is prob-
able that both agencies of the Japanese Government collab-
orate between themselves and with the Japanese Embassy and
Japanese Associations in Argentina.
It 1s

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
It is not unliely that the Solar1 Committee of the
Chamber of Deputies will make use of the enclosed material
in prosecution of the Japanese phase of its Investigation
of Anti-Argentine Activities.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Hagh Milled

Enclosure No. 1 to despatch no. 9366,
dated March 15, 1943, Buenos Aires,
Argentina.
Japan Tourist Bureau, Florida 746, Buenos Aires.
(Letter from)
Argentina Office No. 43 (letter number)
(Copy to Nippon Travel Bureau)
April 17, 1941
Buenos Aires/Office
Tateo TSUTSUMI, in charge
(Addressed to)
Takanori KIMURA, Esquire, Permanent Director,
International Tourist Bureau (name of Japan Tourist Bureau
in Tokyo)
Items Connected with Policy for Project Activities in 194l
In connection with this main subject, your detailed
instructions of February 3, Bureau letter number 59, and
February 5, Bureau letter nunber 45, have been received, and I
trust there will be complete satisfaction with the work of this
office on activities projected for l94l.
There follow a few concrete suggestions of my own for
the plans for work and the hopes of this office:
1. Detailing of a Permanent Resident Bureau
Member to Rio de Janeiro, Brazil
Although the permanent office in Buenos Aires has been
established as the center for propaganda to the various coun-
tries in South America, it cannot be considered as sufficient
by itself for spreading propaganda throughout South America in
view of the tremendous area to be covered, the diversity of the
peoples, and the inconvenience of inter communications. At
present, to make up for this lack, it has been necessary to en-
gage people not on the regular staff to spread propaganda in
various places, or to entrust propaganda work to the Chambers
of Commerce, in other words to give this work to those who know
nothing about it, which I think is the wrong way to spread a
propaganda net. Since the great majority of these people know
practically nothing about the work of the Bureau, not much can
be expected of them, and it all amounts to expense for nothing
on the part of the Bureau.
Rather than have ten part-time propagandists, it must
be clear that one Bureau Office is more effective. Even if
all of the part-time workers, and some of the Chambers of Com-
merce were readjusted, I would want to have this propaganda
organ established (in Rio). For the time being this Permanent
Resident Member of the Bureau in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, should
be under the jurisdiction of the Buenos Aires Office, and it
should probably handle propaganda work directed toward the
northern part of South America.
While Argentina and Brazil are the two largest countries
in South America, their language, customs and peoples are dif-
ferent. If the former is the economic and recently cultural
center

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
center of South America, the latter possesses unique natural
beauty and abundant resources. Even when this office was es-
tablished Rio was a strong candidate, so that I expect that
this plan may soon be realized. I do not believe that the sta-
tioning of a permanent man at Rio will greatly influence the
budget.
2. Propaganda Printed Matter
(a) Attention is invited to the fact that except for
Brazil, Central and South American countries generally use
Spanish, so it goes without argument that most printed propa-
ganda material for this area should be in Spanish. Consider-
able propaganda material has been sent to this office in Portu-
guese, but since Brazil is the only country here using that lang-
uage the material has been practically useless.
(b) A number of different types of publications should
be made up in Spanish. Other than the special study series of
the "Tourist Library" directed to South America, something with-
out such an abundance of articles, and with a wealth of pictures
and illustrations would be better. Not liking difficult things,
something with direct eye appeal should be gotten up for the
people here.
(c) The Spanish edition of the Tourist Library should
be realized as soon as possible. If translation and such is
difficult for the home office, it can be done here.
(d) In general, the lack of good quality paper, the
inexpertness of printing art, etc. locally, makes it impossible
to produce excellent propaganda brochures here. As a result,
this Bureau continually wins praise from these people on its
printed propaganda material. So that I firmly trust that the
quality of paper, etc. will not be lowered in making up propa-
ganda material from now on, in order that it may cope with the
excellent propaganda publications being put out by the British,
Americans, Italians and others.
3. Motion Pictures and Photographs
(a) I have frequently reported on the flourishing con-
dition of the motion picture business here, and it follows as a
fact that motion pictures present the most effective propaganda
medium for these people.
So that it is greatly to be regretted that practically
no motion pictures have been sent to this office since it was
opened. As there has not yet been occasion to have the authori-
ties formally use motion pictures, I take this opportunity to
request that several Spanish editions of films be made up quickly
and sent here post haste. Of course it is best to have the films
with sound announcements on them, but if it is difficult for
Tokyo to make them, then if you send them plain or without dub-
bing, I can have the sound track put on here and the necessary
number of copies made. I should like to say that you should not
regard

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
regard this as a backward country for whether rightly or wrongly
it thinks of itself as being far superior to Japan in culture,
living, etc. So that I would add that you should send movies
which are about the equal of those which you send to Europe and
America.
(b) In order to deepen the cognizance of love for the
mother country on the part of the second generation Japanese
born in Argentina, if there are some suitable films among those
made by the Traffic Department, they should be sent. I will
get the cooperation of our official establishments, the Japanese
Associations, etc. in having these films shown in various places.
(c) The scope for using the show window facing the
street for a general exhibition of pictures is quite large, so
that a half dozen or more photographs should be sent at one time.
Also I would like to have Japan Photo Service pictures sent.
4. Advertisements in Newspapers and Magazines.
Here there is a British-American group and a German-
Italian group of newspapers. The German-Italien group has
hitherto been very friendly in articles printed, and this office
intends to form even closer association with these papers in the
future. But the British-American group is quite different. But
its circulation network is extremely extensive, and I believe
it would be very effective if our country could use that network
for spreading our tourist propaganda. However, in view of the
present international situation that will not be an easy thing
to do. Last year our Embassy (at that time a Legation) and others
were working with La Nación in preparation of a 26o0th Year of
the Founding of the Japanese Empire Anniversary Number, a Japanese
propaganda edition, when bang, the whole business was suspended,
it is said that that was due to the interference of a third coun-
try. Since then I have been thinking of various ways of edging
into the propaganda editions of these powerful newspapers. Of
course it would be difficult to get them to take straight polit-
ical propaganda, but by turns it might be possible to get them
to use cultural and sightseeing propaganda. For the present as
a concrete measure I would like to have them carry our pictures,
etc. in the weekly rotogravure sections of papers like La Prensa
and La Nación.
5. Radio Broadcasts
As I have already reported in connection with radio
broadcasting here, ever since the establishment of this office,
I have continuously been putting something into operation, and
in view of the power of radio propaganda I shall continue to do
so in the future. I believe that broadcasting methods will
gradually improve. I wish that you would on occasion send me
some new records.
6. Propaganda Exhibits
As you know, this office has two front show windows,
and inside there is a big hall and arcade which can be used to
exhibit

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
exhibit propaganda material, so I wish to have some sent. Now
and for some time past I have awaited the speedy arrival of the
skilled arts pictures requested. Also I want several dioramas
to use for exhibition at other times.
7. Invitations to Foreigners
As this country is the one fartherest away from Japan,
anyone from here visiting Japan must expect to be away at least
four months to do so. So that leaving the present international
situation aside, an ordinary person in business using for instance
the summer vacation, cannot very easily accomplish such a visit.
For that reason, in the future rather than extending invitations
to people like school mistresses and those tied down by fixed
jobs, it would be better to invite those with their time com-
paratively free such as literary men, newspaper writers, artists,
etc. And this office would especially like to obtain collabora-
tion with those quarters.
In conclusion, aside from the above-mentioned invitations
to foreigners I believe that it is today increasingly important
to strengthen the recognition for the home country of the second
generation Japanese living here. So that in the future I want to
work with our official establishments in selecting promising young
second generation boys and girls to send to the mother country on
inspection trip parties, etc., and I would like to obtain the
assistance of the home office in organizing this into a reality.
THE END
GW:mo

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure 3 to despatch 9366,
Mar. 15, 1943, Buenos Aires.
JAPANESE TOURIST BUREAU BUDGET
CONFIDENTIAL
Details and Application of the l94l Ludget Plan
I. Receipts
(1) General Accounts Subsidy.
In revising the Yen 500,000 subsidy for this
fiscal year (1940) an increase of Yen 100,000 was requested,
but after repeated negotiations with the Finance Ministry au-
thorities, not alone was the requested increase not recognized
as a principle, but following the general subsidy curtailment
policy, another Yen loo,Oo0 was cut off, leaving only a sub-
sidy of Yen 4o0,Oo0 within which to work.
(2) Supporting Members Contributions
At first a s ubscription of Yen 800,o00 was requested
from the Railway Special Accounts, but in view of the down-
ward revision made by the Finance .inistry, an increase of
Yen 50,ooo was requested and obtained, making the subscription
come to Yen 850,000.
In these items the "others" comes mainly from
Kansai (around Osaka) and Kanto (around Tokyo) traffic people.
(3) Miscellaneous Receipts
In this item the "Nippon Travel Bureau Share" was
appropriated to cover the travel and other necessary expenses
of despatching a Nippon Travel Bureau man to New York, Rome,
Buenos Aires, Manila and Hanoi, and instead was handed over
by that Bureau to become a receipt here. (In the expenditure
column it appears under propaganda expenses for those dele-
gated to part time duty).
II. Expenditures.
(1) Business Expenses.
Under this item the "Salaries and Wages" was to
have provided for the employment of three more persons to add
to the twenty-seven now employed by the Bureau, but as the bud-
get is the same as before, they will not be added.
In November of this year (194l) the necessary ex-
penses for the Third East Asia Sightseeing Conference, to be
sponsored in Manila, will come out of the "Foreign Travel"
and the"East Asia Sightseeing Conference Expense" items.
(2) Overseas Offices Expenses.
In Europe, in response to the changes in the inter-
national situation, the Paris office was closed, as well as
the Railway Ministry's Office there to which propaganda work
had been entrusted, and the personnel was transferred to New
York. In place of the London office the Berlin office was
newly

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
owly established, and it took over general propaganda directed
toward Europe, and in keeping with the Tri-Partite Pact be-
tween Japan, Germany and Italy, undertook to bring into close
coalition Japanese and German Tourist work.
In order to strengthen the ties of the East Asia
Co-prosperity Sphere, and to contribute to the advancement of
friendly concert between the various countries in the Sphere,
an office was newly established in Hano1, French Indo-China.
New appropriations were made for both offices.
Hitherto travelling expenses among the expense
1tems allotted for the overseas offices have been borne half
by this office (The International Tourist Bureau) and half by
the Japan Travel Bureau, and appropriations made accordingly.
But beginning with the 194l fiscal year (April 1, 194l through
Larch 31, 1942) the expenses of the overseas offices will be
charged only to the International Tourist Bureau. Expenses
borne together with the Japan Travel Bureau have been changed
to the appropriation for Special Delegated Propaganda Expenses.
Details for each office follow. Attention 1s called
to fact that the personnel fund is about normal, but that should
not be taken to mean that salaries are to be increased or more
new people hired.

(3) Propaganda Expenses
Tota1 Yen 843,000 (1,046,000
in 1940)
This year's appropriation is a decrease of Yen
203,000 from last year. The principal reasons for this as
have already been stated in the Projects Activity Policy, are
that in accompanying the change in the international situation,
there has been a decrease of propaganda directed towards the
United States, and the United States has suspended the issuance
of passports to travelers to the Far East, etc., in view of
which the number of invitations to foreigners (to visit Japan)
has been checked.
The various items under propaganda expenditures
are explained as follows:
(a) Printed propaganda material
Yen 225,000 (23,000 in
1940)
The principal things planned for this year are as
follows:
General guide booklets in various formats,
the same as this year (1940) in German, Ita-
lian, Spanish, Portuguese, Anamese and other
languages.
Continuation of the Tourist Library Series as
hitherto, and selection of some of those already
published for translation into German.
Continuation of Travel in Japan after scrutiny
of the contents, and publication of a special
number in German and Italian.
Combined publication of the Guide to Japan and
the Graphic into a folder style photographic
guide for backward countries.
It is planned to bear part of the joint publi-
cation expense for the magazine Introduction to
Japan and Manohukuo being planned by the German
Propaganda Ministry.
(b) Motion

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
(b) Motion pictures
Yen 165,000 (130,000 in
1940)
Plans have been made for the production of two
two-reel pictures for European distribution, one three-reel
picture for the Greater East Asia Co-prosperity Sphere, and
one one-reeler for general distribution, suitably sound tracked
according to the context in English, German, French, Spanish,
Portuguese, Chinese, Malayan, and Anamese.
Imported film will be used for making duplicates
for European distribution, while it is planned to use Japanese-
made film for those going to backward countries.
As 16 mm reduced editions (both with and without
sound) compare with 35 mm film, still more is the importance to
be attached to natural color films. But inasmuch as color equip-
ment has not yet been set up in this country, it is planned to
obtain the assistance of suitable technicians such as Agfa to
set this up. (In view of the International situation Eastman
Kodak Company has postponed setting up such equipment in Japan).

Analysis of Certain 'aterial Contained in the Takano

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have/the honor to invite the Department's attention to
P
the Legal Attaché's communications  o. 1750'of December 11 and
IS
No. 1754 of December 16, 1942, and to the letter in reply from
Washington dated February 27, 1943, concerning certain material
obtained by thd Legal Attaché's office from the files of the
Japanese CO!PAIA CHILENA ORIENTAL, a firm representing the
MITSUI interests in Chile.
Although the Compañfa Chilena Oriental was ostensibly
interested primarily in the importation of textiles from Japan,
APR
the principal concern of the company appears to have been
minerals needed by the Japanese war industry. Furthermore,
2
this firm was for a long time suspected of being engaged in
1
espionage activities. This suspicion was given weight in August
6
'1942 by the disclosure that the expulsion from Chile of GORO
MIYASkI, anager of the Compañfa Chilena Oriental, had been
ordered by the Minister of the Interior for the stated reason
that his immigration papers were not in order but actually be-
cause reports of the Dirección General de Investigaciones had
proven him to be a spy. As reported in my strictly confidential
despatch No. 4296/ dated August 28, 1942, the person who informed
the Embassy regarding this action, a high official of Investigacionas
said that he believed Miyasaki to have been the most dangerous
Japanese espionage agent in Chile.
894.20225960
An examination of page 3 of the material obtained by Mr.
Wall and subsequently translated by his Home Office would lead
one at first sight to the opinion that officials of the Com
pañfa Chilena Oriental had foreknowledge of the impending
attaok on Pearl Harbor. In this connection reference is made
to the items noted under the date December 5 Order to evacuate
from

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
from the head of the personnel department"
 . . "(2) methods of liquidating the company." However, it
appears that TAk NO and the other officials of the company could
not have known that the Japanese Navy would attack as early as
December 7 in view of certain plans made by the company on
December 5 as stated in items (3) and (4) of page 3: "(3) state-
rooms on the Tatmuta Maru ordered by the San Francisco office"
and "(4) Made reservation for Panama by airplane (leaving the
21st) and by steamship (leaving the 13th)."
It will be noted that in general the Takano file deals with
Japanese interest in the availability to Japan of strategic war
materials in Chile and in the stooks of strategic minerals and
the production of war materials in the United States. Of more
specific interest, however, is the revelation that officials of
the Compafifa Chilena Oriental were in close contact with Mr.
"MO" immediately prior to his departure for the United States in
November 1942. According to the Takano file (photostat Nos. 178-231),
HAGIWARA and NAKATSUKA met with Mr. O on October 26, 1942, and made
a request of him, probably with regard to a cable reoeived from the
Oriental Cotton Company of Japan; on October 28 Takano received
word fromn this person about a certain matter; on October 29 and
November 10 officials of the company visited the home of Mr. MO;
and on October 29 Hagiwara and Takano deposited in the Japanese
Legation one hundred receipts from r. MO. As the Department is
aware, Ir. MO has been positively identified as Rafael MORENO
Echevarria, anti-American and pro-German Chilean Deputy who has
been in the pay of the Japanese Government for acting as its
agent.
There are other items of possi ble interest to the Depart-
ment in the written material in qestion. As early as September
1, 1942, Takano was taking a pessimistic view of Japan's position
with regard to Chile because of his anxiety over the forthooming
break in diplomatic relations. On the other hand, with character-
istic Japanese optimism, in the "Survey of Amerioan Munitions
Industry" dated July 7, 1942, in the seotion dealing with man-
ganese, it is stated t hat although it is not likely that there
will be a shortage of manganese in the United States until 1965,
since sixty per cent of this mineral will from that year on have
to be imported from overseas, in the face of a blockade the United
States would be in a serious position with regard to manganese.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Donald R. Heath,
Counselor of Embassy

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.98)
DIVISION OF
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF THE AMERICAN REPUBLICS
THE AMERICAN REPURLICS
May 5, 1943
DEPARTMENT OF A
RA - Mr. Dr1ey:
Mr. Bonsal:
STAIE
O.S.S. REPORT "FTELD NOTES ON THE
JAPANESE TH. LATIN AMERICA
Attached is a reply
signature.
With respect to the report itself, I think it may
be said that it contains comparatively little informa-
FW 894.20210/220
tion not already available in the Department in.the
form of reports from Mr. Emmerson in Lima, Mr. Cabot
Coville and Mr. Lynn Smith.
However, the O.S.S. report has two definite
adventages. First of all, it is an excellent brief
summary of Japanese immigration, population and
economic activities in the other American republics,
and of the controls established by the Japanese Gov-
ernment and by Japanese corporations over Japanese
immigrants and their second generation descendents.
Secondly, this report presents an excellent and
careful anelysis of population statistics with respect
to Japanese in the other American republics. This is
scarcely surprising since it appears that its author
is Robert B. Hall, Professor of Geogrephy at the
University of Michigan, who has specialized in the
894
study of Japan and the Japanese. I can safely say that
I have seen no more compact and carefully presented
MAY 1 7.1943
study on any foreign minority in the other American
FILED
republics. In addition to text and tables, there are
a number of excellent maps.
One interesting point made in the report is that
there are among the Japanese in Latin America, and
especially in Peru, a number of Okinawans, or natives
of the Loo Choo Archipelago. The report emphasizes the
fact that these people are somewhat despised by the "true
Japanese and that they feel certain resentment on that
account. On pages 37-38 and 65-66 there are suggestions
that it might be possible to take advantage of this fact
in our propaganda and thereby weaken the unity among
the Japanese, especially in Peru, and to a certain ex-
tent in Brazil.
LLJET
RA:JEJ:VLH

MEMORANDUI
Re: COMPANIA MINERO PETROLERA DEL PACIFICO
Republic of Ecuador
The Compania Minero Petrolera del Pacifico is an Ecuadoran corporation
and appears to be principally owned by Colombians and to have originated in
Bogota. The company was founded in March, 1940 and grew out of concessions
acquired in August, 1939. These concessions were the oil rights to 2,100,000
hectares of land along the coast of the Province of Esmeraldas, in Ecuador.
22
The officers of the company were Luis Matinez Delgado, President; Marco
Antopio Aulle, Vice-President; Gustavo Diez Delgado, Manager; Enrique
Mosquera Wallis, Assistant Manager; and Carlos Meroado, Secretary. Diez
Delgado is an Ecuadoran, and a nephew of President Arroyo del Rio. Mercado
is also an Ecuadoran, but the remainder of the officers are Colombians from
Bogota. However, Luis Martinez Delgado has resided in Ecuador since 1938.
According to the informant, it has been a matter of general knowledge
that the International Petroleum Company, Ltd., has been interested in
buying the concession held by the Compania Minero Petrolera del Pacifico for
some time. The informant states a reliable source has advised that although
there have been many reports of Japanese interests in the company, as yet
no definite information or proof has been developed that the Japanese had any
interest in it. This source advises, however, that information had been
received at the United States Embassy in Bogota from a source believed reliable
to the effect that the Compania Minero Petrolera del Pacifico was controlled
by a Japanese trust and that it was financed by Japanese interests. No details
were set forth as to the trust or the manner of financing. The source further
advised that several other communications to the same effect had been received
from Bogota, but since they did not state specific information and seemed
to be based on rumor, it has not been agreed to give them any credence.
He further stated that in the pending transaction,unls
specific evidence was produced, the Compania Minero Petroler de Pa
would be treated as if no enemy interests were involved, wi texcoption
Confident
that a clause was to be placed in the contract guaranteeingon the part of
the contractors that none of the money should in any event go to subjects
of the Axd.s.
The source of the above information further staate had informatio
to the effect that the Japanese Legation had interested itself in the Compania
Minero Petrolera del Pacifico when it received information that the company
could not afford to hire American geologists. Upon receiving this informa-
Te
tion the Japanese Minister had offered to obtain Japanese geologists at a
lower price than Americans could be paid, and it was stated that Japanese
geologists were hired through his intervention. In regard to Martines Delgado
and Dies Delgado, the source advised he had never received any information
to the effect that these individuals were in any way sympathetic to Japan
or to the Axis.

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
JDUM
Page 2
The informant also advised that another reliable source has stated
that since 1942 the officials of the Compania Minero Petrolera del Pacifico,
principally Luis Martinez Delgado and Gustavo Diez Delgado, and the officials
of the International Petroleum Company had been in agreement upon a contract
whereby the International Petroleum Company was to pay the Compania Minero
Petrolera del Pacifico the sum of $100,000.00 for the concession on the
signing of legal papers and a royalty of two cents per barrel on the first
ten million barrels and one cent per barrel on all the petroleum taken out
thereafter. According to the source, the Delgados are very anxious to
cloge the deal because they have no money with which to pay S/8o,ooo in
taxes coming due on the concession in March, 1943 and they fear a forfeiture
of the lease. According to the informant, all that was needed to culminate
the transaction was the final approval of President Arroyo del Rio.
The source further stated that the International Petroleum Company
had conducted investigations in Ecuador, Bogota and the United States to
determine whether the Japanese held an interest in the company, and as yet,
they had been able to find no evidence of such ownership. He indicated,
however, that he was by no means sure that they did not have such an
interest and said he was suspicious because the officers of the Compania
Minero Petrolera del Pacifico had hired Japanese geologists and had given
as their reason that American geologists wanted more money than the company
could afford to pay. He further stated that he had had an opportunity to
examine the sums paid to the Japanese geologists, and it appeared that
they were paid as much as American geologists would have been paid for the
same work. Therefore, said the informant, it did not appear that the
Japanese geologists had been hired for financial reasons.
The source pointed out an incident which might possibly bear on
the question of Japanese capital in the company. He stated that in June
of 1940, he was travelling from Balboa to Miami when he was accosted by
an American, a total stranger, who said that he represented the Japanese
banking house of Mitsui in Panama and that the Mitsuis were interested in
acquiring rice and oil lands in Ecuador. He told the source that he
would pay him one dollar per hectare for any land that he was instrumental
in assisting him to acquire. He asked the source to stop at the offices of
the Mitsui interests in New York City for further directions. He stated
that he later consulted the president of his (the source's) company and did
not have anything further to do with the Mitsui proposition. The source
suggests that Compania Minero might have received a similar offer and that
the Japanese interests might have been in the nature of an option, pending
the report of their geologists.
The following information has been received from a confidential
reliable source in Bogota, Colombia concerning the Compania Minero Petrolera
del Pacifico:
A confidential reliable source stated that the company in question
was founded August 1, 1939 and was originated by Martines Delgado, Gustavo
Dies Delgado, and Sr. Mosquera. It also appears that one Manuel Topres
Peralta, former secretary to the Colombian Minister of Mines and Petroleum,

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
ORANDUM
Page 3
and presently employed as a landman for the Tropical Oil Company, was also
interested in the company. However, it appears that Torres lost his interest
in the company and its concession in Ecuador through the failure to meet
a payment of 23,000.
According to another source, in January, 1940 Torres went to the
Republic of Ecuador, where he obtained with Martinez Delgado an enormous oil
concession consisting of approximately 4,000,000 hectares, as pointed out
above. Torres lost his concession through the failure to meet the above-
mentioned payment. According to the source, this same concession is the
one which was later obtained by Japanese interests. The Japanese interests
appeared to have arranged with the interested parties, who could not raise
40,000 Colombian pesos, to meet obligations to the Ecuadoran government.
It is said that the Japanese paid the current obligations and that the
negotiations were held in Bogota in the office of one Sugamami and another
Japanese called Nakamura. Nakamura is said to have been the one who actually
paid off in order to keep the whole matter secret. The deal, according to
the source, was legalized in Quito, Ecuador with the assistance of a lawyer
named Ayora. It is said that the Japanese then sent geologists to Ecuador
and mapped the concession. The source is of the opinion that Martinez
Delgado, if not actually presently representing the Japanese, certainly did
so until recently.
In further connection with the negotiations with the Japanese, the
source stated that the payment in question was made in May  June of 1940
and that he believes the Japanese paid in the neighborhood of 500,000 pesos.
It is not known whether the 40,000 pesos used to pay the Ecuadoran government
obligations was extra or was included in the original amount. The contact
man and the individual who seemed to have first interested the Japanese in
the deal is believed to be a former Lieutenant Colonel in the Colombian Army
Intelligence by the name of Felis Castillo Marino. It appears that Castillo
started to interest the Japanese in the proposition during the latter part
of l939, and it is believed that he received a commission for his work in
the transaetion. According to the source, the money paid by the Japanese
was probably paid in the presence of Luis Martinez Delgado on the one side
and Nakamura on the other. It is said to be a positive fact that the meeting
actually took place in Suganami's office in the Bolsa Building in Bogota.
About this time Martines Delgado had been seen in several public conversa-
tions with Suganami and had appeared with him on numerous occasions in the
Hotel Granada.
The files of the confidential source reflect in a memorandum
dated November 13, 1940, that a bill had been prepared for the National
Congress of Colombia jointly by the Minister of Labor and the Minister of
National Economy which would permit the Colombian government to import
rayon directly from Japan and to sell the rayon to private interests.
According to the memerandum, the three Japandse supposedly behipd this
idea were Toranosuke Nakamura, Tadao Nakmmurasand Truneso Yago, all of
whom had arrived in Colombia from Quayaquil, Eouador in October, 1940.

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
ANDUM
Page 4
The files also reveal that one K. Brandoh arrived in Barranquilla from
Bogota February 16, 194l and that he was the secrstary of the Nippon
Trading Agency in Bogota. This individual was apparently connected with
S. Suganami, who had been in Colombia for fifteen years and was the agent
for the Nippon Trading Agency.
The following is a sumary of the information presently available
regarding Shimichi Suganami, who is apparently identical with the Japanese
individual of the same name concerned in this case. His address in Bogota
was Bolsa de Bogota and his occupation, that of agent of the Nippon Trade
Agency. Information was received in August, 1940 that Suganami was born in
Tokyo January 26, 1887 and is a Buddhist. He possessed Passport #214632 and
#113877, the first bearing the date January 16, 1933 and the latter January
21, 1929. It was stated he arrived in Bogota in 1930 to remain for fifteen
days. He allegedly works for the Japanese government and had been in
Colombia four times, the first being in 1928. In December, 1940 a report
was received that the Japanese were endeavoring to purchase scrap iron in
Colombia for Mitsui interests in Japan and that Suganami was the Bogota
agent. In February, 1938 it was stated that Suganami was believed to be
a Japanese Chamber of Commerce representative and possibly connected with
the Japanese government. It was stated that he made an inquiry at the
Tropical Oil Company concerning the possibility of making large purchases,
and repeated the inquiry in October of 1937.
In an undated memorandum, it is reflected that a confidential
reliable source had advised that shortly after his arrival in Bogota, he
encountered a Japanese whose face was familiar. He later recalled that
this individual had been employed in the British Consulate in Manila and
had been implicated in an important espionage case there. He identified
the Japanese as Sugmnami, who was then the manager of the Nippon Trading
Agency and a frequent guest at the home of the Japanese Charge d'Affaires.
It was further stated that it is believed that Suganami was a captain in
the Japanese military service at the time of the Manila incident and that
it was presumed that he was still a member of the Japanese intelligence
service.
In December, 1941 a report was received that Suganami had just
registered with the police, giving his address as the Japanese Legation.
It was indicated that the Legation was then under surveillance by the
police. Final notation indicates that Suganami was deported April 10, 1942.
In connection with Suganami's departure, it is interesting to note that
the Japanese who were being deported from Buenaventura on April 18, 1942
aboard the SS Acadia raised every possible objection to leaying the country,
but finally received the necessary departure orders direct from Tokyo. It
is stated that Suganami even went to the extreme of professing a desire to
become a Catholic and tried to have Catholic leaders intercede for him. His
request, however, was refused.

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
ORANDUM
Page 5
The following data have been obtained from a confidential source
concerning Luis Martinez Delgado. In an undated memorandum, it was stated
that Luis Martinez Delgado was an active defender of Nazism, and that he
is capable of entering into any kind of business enterprise which would
be beneficial to Germany. It was stated that he had had business with
Nazis and Fascists and that these individuals had put him at the head of
the Compania Minero Petrolera del Pacifico in order to work with the
Japanese, who have direct connection with them. On March 16, 1943 a
report was received indicating that Jose Tibau y Duran, Chancellor of the
Spanish Legation, Quito, Ecuador, frequently came from Quito to Tulcan,
where he always stayed with Delgado, "who acts as mailing agent for Carmelite
Friars in that area."
On April 10, 1942 a reliable informant reported that the Righmond
Petroleum Company had become interested in an oil development project on
the coast of Ecuador near the Colombian border. It is stated that rather
than use the company name, the Richmond Petroleum Company had used the
name of a Colombian, Luis Martinez Delgado, and a Venezuelan by the name
of Torres. The informant stated that both men had made plane trips to the
locale in Ecuador and had been reimbursed by Richmond. The source stated
the Richmond Petroleum Company suddenly lost interest in the development,
inasmuch as they heard it was not a good petroleum field. Afterwards,
however, the venture was reestablished and the new company was organized,
under the name of Luis Martinez Delgado, without the participation of
Torres, Richmond, or any American or British oil companies. The informant
stated he suspected that either Japanese or German interest have financed
Luis Martinez Delgado in this venture, since they are the most logical
groups to be interested in oil developments or a possible base on the
west coast of South America.
The informant further stated that the reason he suspects some
connection between Japanese interests and Martinez Delgado is the fact
that the latter has had for some time very friendly relations with the
Nippon Trading Agency, whose offices are on the sixth floor of the Edificio
de la Bolsa.
Considerable information has been reported concerning the
Compania Minero Petrolera del Pacifico suggesting that Japanese are
involved in the company. However, most of the reports are reiterations
of the above and are not considered worthy of mention at this time. Furth
investigation is being made in an endeavor to obtain information either
confirming or nullifying the allegations of Japanese interests in this
company.

SLCRET
GENERAL
Two factors mitigate against the successful conduct
of espionage by the Japanese in Latin America. The first of
these factors is that of racial physiognomy applicable to the
Japanese the world over, and particularly to Latin America
where this race has never been really socially or racially
assimilated into Latin American Nationals. The second factor -
that twenty of the twenty-one atin American nations have
broken relations with or declared war on Japan - when coupled
with the resulting social and geographical isolation, com-
.202
bine to almost completely impede the Japanese - as Japanese -
in such effort.
Frozen into a national unity by these factors and
under the control of the Japanese Overseas Ministry, the
Japanese National has become entrenched in Japanese govern-
ment controlled, financed and purposed, small industries,
and are compelled to conduct their espionage under the cloak
of Spanish cover names and seemingly small middle class
businesses. Ostensibly individual and unconnected entities,
these Japanese Nationals are actually woven into an inte-
grated espionage network which funnels information to their
onepresently open control point and clearing house, the
Japanese Embassy at Buenos Aires, Argentina.
BACKGROUND
Confidential File
Information has recently been received which
develops the "Japsa" Espionage Ring as being operated
principally in Argentina, Brazil and Chile. Inasmuch as
the Japanese Embassy at Santiago, Chile, flourished openly
until the break of relations in January, 1942, that city
to that time was the intermediary coordination point for
the collection of espionage information for re-transmittal

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
4
to Japan. The control and operation of this system was
originally cloaked under the Compania Chilena Oriental, with
alias Sogledad Commercial Chilena Oriental, the representative
of the Mitsui interests for Chile.
Functioning in a coordinated effort with this cover
company was the Compania Taibo, with alias The Pacific Trading
Company. Both of these Mitsui comnanies, having branches under
varied Spanish names throughout Latin America, were still
further coordinated with the Nambei Trading Company, the Iwei
Company and the Oriental Cotton Wool Company, at Buenos Aires.
Upon the breaking of relations by Chile with the Axis on
January 20, 1?42, the Nambei Trading Company and others at
Buenos Aires became the main focal point for the reception
of information and its re-transmission to Japan. As an
illustration of this, it is known that the Japanese Ambassador
at Buenos Aires has informed Tokyo as follows:
"Various studies are being made of the
proper way. of establishing an organization
of research on conditions in the United States.
In view of the fact that there has been a
suppression of Fifth Column activities and
invigoration of pro-Anglo-American elements,
we are afraid numerous merchants going in
and out of this office would arouse public
criticism. Since the beginning of the war
we have added six persons to the staff in
the U. S. research (Yoshioa of litsui; Liyakawa
of Asano; Izawa of Iwai; Tanabe of Okura; Ogawa
of Shoshen; and Suzuki of Kahematsu). More-
over, Suda of Iwai and Fuiri of Itochu have
been working on South American research. (I
have always had an understanding with the
proper heads of the branch offices.) (These
eight men are now working on secret materials,
and it is again necessary to take over em-
ployees from private business for office work.)
I would like to appoint these eight persons
to the position of shokutaku."
The position of "shokutaku" is the official title
for a specialist attached to a diplomatic mission who is

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
not an employee of the regular roreign service staff. The
Asano mentioned above is the Asano Bussan (Asano Products
Company); Iwai is Iwax Shoten; Okura is Okura - Ministry
of France; Shoshen is Osaka Shoshen Kabushiki Kaisha (Osaka
Steamship Company). In addition, it is known that the
Japanese Ambassador was empowered by the Japanese Foreign
Office to use the personnel of Litsui for investigative
operations concerning American conditions, and also was
allowed to employ other Japanese mercantile personnel in
a similar manner.
PERSONNEL
COMPANIA CHILENA ORIENTAL
Reliable information has been developed that the
Compania Chilena Oriental at Santiago, Chile, is being used
as a cover for the obtainance of espionage information. It
has been ascertained that this firm was originally established
under the name of S. Shuakata in 1935. SI UAKATA himself is
presently believed to be in Buenos Aires, reputedly in charge
of the Mitsui interests at that point. The firm so named was
changed to the Compania Chilena Oriental in 1937 but the
Japanese principals of the company still remained as Mitoui
and Company of Japan. The principal exports of this firm
were ostensibly copper ores, wool, manganese and Chilean
prodyee (barley, oats, wheat, etc.). Two Japanese, KANAME
NAKYAMA and SUCYUKE NAKATSUKA, are reported to have a joint
ownership and interest in the firm. The declared capital of
this company is one hundred twenty thousand dollars (pesos),
but they have operated reputedly in millions of pesos until
the outbreak of the current war. Reputedly, this firm has
in the neighborhood of twenty-five million dollars (pesos)
presently in Chile, which amounts are in the hands of third
parties, but more especially under the control of ISAO TAKADA,
a representative of the Yokohama Specie Pank. . Also employed
by this company at the present time are TAKEO TAKANO, SUSUKI
NAKATSUKA and S. HAGB!ARA, as present managers to replace
one GORO MIYAsAKI. An additional confidential employee of
tris company 1s TAKEO AZASA TANAKA.
Additional information follows regarding this
personnel.

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)

ISAO TAKADA
TAKADA, a Japanese National and representative of
the Yokohama Specie Bank, resides at Calle Monjitas 560,
Santiago in the same apartment with NAKAYAMA, the Japanese
Naval Attache. It is reported that TAKADA is financially
reimbursing most of the Japanese who appear to be collabo-
rating with the Japanese Ministry, and that the vast majority
of them usually manage to maintain contact with TAKADA, either
personally or through third parties. TAKADA is considered by
the Japanese Legation as an individual of considerable impor-
tance and an expert in both the banking and economic fields.
In addition to being representative of the Yokohama Specie
Bank, TAKADA acts as Chief Financial Advisor to the Nippon
Trade Agency. TAKADA has been reported a number of times as
acting secretary for the Japanese Military Attache, and is
krown to have been present with Japanese Military Attache
TFSINA at a meeting with a number of German espionage sus-
pects at the Munich Cafe in Penaflor on July 19, 1942, where,
it is stated, plans were made for Japanese and German collab-
oration. TAKADA is described as follows:

KANAME NAKAYAMA NAKAYAMA
NAKAYAMA presently resides at Avenida Bulnes 80,
Apartment 84, Santiago. In addition to his position with this
company, he is reported as a representative of the Japanese
firm Yoya Lianka Kanisha Ldta. It is also known that although
NAKAYAMA is married to NOBUKA KOBAYASHI MIYANEHI, who left
for Japan on August 18, 1941, he is living with an unknown
young woman recently arrived from Argentina, who is believed
to be his secretary. He is the holder of Brazilian passport
No. O16216 issued at Sao Paulo, Brazil, September 13, 1939.
NAKAYAMA left Kobe, Japan, on March 23, 1935, arrived in
Rio de Janerio in June of the same year, left Santos, Brazil,
on September 18, 1939, from where he journeyed to Santiago,
Chile, via Buenos Aires, arriving in October of the same
year. His Japanese passport is No. Ol286.

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
NAKAYAMA has made a special effort to ingratiate
himself with Chilean society, and to some extent has achieved
success. He has endeavored to develop friendships among
important governmental and army officials, and through these
individuals he has been able to photograph many important
installations. It might be noted at this point that NAKAYAMA
is the owner of a complete set of very expensive photographic
equipment, which includes two of the best modern Leica
cameras.
NAKAYAMA, it is said, is the son of a wealthy Japanese
who is a member of several large Japanese industrial concerns
in Japan, and it is believed that because of type and volume
of. his contacts that he is also charged with a portion of the
Mitsui fund dispersals through the Japanese Consulate. NAKA-
YAMA has never hesitated to boast of his former affiliation
with the Japanese Army as a Captain and his speciality as a
commercial draftsman artist. He is known, however, to be
somewhat contemptuous of his fellow Japanese employees in the
company, who, it is stated, treat this individual with consider-
able respect.
During February of 1940 NAKAYAl'A began to assemble
aerlal photographs of the Chilean coastal regions, with par-
ticular attention to terrain details. It is known that he
endeavored at that time to amass a collection of these photo-
graphs which it was believed he obtained from the Linea Aerea
Nacional, the Chilean Air Line.
NAKAYALA's interests, however, extend to other sub-
jects, and it is reliably reported that as an expert drafts-
man he has made accurate maps of the coal mine districts of
the south of Chile. In addition, reputedly, he has made a
complete study of coal and copper mining in Chile. He has
stated that his investigations and interest in these matters
were prompted by his ultimate intention of forming a large
scale Japanese Chilean firm to exploit these mines in Chile.
During the period that GORO MIYASAKI acted as Manager
of the Company, it appeared that NAKAYAMA served more or less
as his private secretary and intimate friend, travelling in
the same general social circles. It is known that in addition

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
to receiving salary payments in Santiago, Nakayama, in the
position of being an agent of the South American Cotton Com-
pany of Dallas and Santiago received his May and June, 194l,
salary totalling $3l2.00, through the Sao Paulo, Brazil, office
of the company. The correspondence forwarding this payment,
copies of which were furnished to New York and Dallas, stated
that NAKAYAMA's May salary had been paid by the Osaka main
office in Japan, but that future salary would come from the
New York office at the rate of $156 per month. It is to be
noted that a letter from the Sao Paulo office dated May 19,
1941, advised the Santiago office that separate from the
salary question the Sao Paulo office desired to know why
the Santiago office requested 40,000 or $50,000 from a cir-
culating fund maintained in the Dallas office. The inquiry
requested that the withdrawal would depend on how and when
it was to be repaid, and who was to receive it.

GORO MIYASAKI
This individual, former manager of the Compania
Chilena Oriental, is presently living in Cordoba, Argentina,
as a concession from his Argentine Government Internment
Processo, which ordered him interned in the Plaza Hotel,
Cordoba, Argentina. This individual was born in Shizueka,
Japan, on July 29, 1903. MIYASAKI has practically commuted
throughout lower Latin America during the last eight years,
with his main headquarters at Santiago, Chile, where he
was manager of the Compania Chilena Oriental. As an
example of Subject's Japanese intelligence activities, it
is known that MIYASAKI arranged for and planned the robbery
against the American Cultural Attache at Santiago, Chile,
on the part of Alira Inostrozo in the belief that the

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.99)
-7~
Cultural Attache was the Intelligence Officer of the American
Embassy and as a consequence documents valuable to the Japanese
would be so procured. A further example of his connections
is in the fact that a close collaborator with MIYASAKI was
one HANS POCHERS, formerly German Consul General at New York
City, presently interned at Zapallar, Chile.
On September 15, 1942, the Chilean government failed
to renew the passport of MIYASAKI, who was forced to flee to
Argentina. LIYASAKI was arrested by the Mendoza, Argentina
Police as a Japanese espionage agent and undesirable person.
Since MIYASAKI's move to Buenos Aires, it has become known
that he is associated with and used as a cover the Nambei
Export and Import Company, San Martin 379, Buenos Aires.
Voluminous reports have been received from reliable
sources to the effect that from 1936 on, MIYASAKI, protected
by his commercial activities, has carried on an active espion-
age campaign in favor of Japan. It is said that it was the
result of the public disclosure of these activities that prompted
MIYAShKI's move to Chile where his activities were continued.
As an example of Subject's travels, the records show
that IYASAKI has a passport granted by the Japanese Consul
in Buenos Aires in February of 194l, which is followed by a
six month's visa by the Chilean Corfsulate at that point. In
March of 194l MIYASAKI went by air to Bolivia and again ob-
tained a further visa for Chile from the Peruvian government
on April 27, 194l. His passport was re-visaed by the
Argentine Consul at Santiago, Chile, on September 1l, 1942,
in order to provide for his being expelled from the country.
A review of the reports received shows that in
addition to the supervisory duties which MIYASAKI had with
the Compania Chilena Oriental while in Chile, he was also
very active with the direction of general Japanese activities
through the intermediary of the Nippon Trade Agency and the
Club de Residentes of Santiago, ru by one MIYOSHI SHIGO
SOZAN, a member of the Overseas Ministry, as a medium of
Japanese propaganda. As a further example of Subject's
activities, during the course of one of MIYASAKI's trips,
his luggage was searched at Arica, Chile, by agents of the
Department of Investigations and a number of maps of ports,

-8-
airports, coastal defenses and mineral regions were removed
from his luggage.

TAKEO GAKUO TAKANO
This individual, one of the present managers of the
Compania Chilena Oriental, was born in Yokohama, Japan, in
1904. Information has been supplied that this individual,
acting as secretary for the company, is the actual coordinator
of economic intelligence information received by the company,
and in addition is known to work for the Japanese Legation.
An example of this latter is the fact that he himself has
noted that on November 14 to 16, 1942, he worked all day send-
ing by cable to Japan, the report on the survey of metals in
America.
TAKANO left Tokyo, Japan, on March 8, 1940, and
arrived in San Francisco, California, enroute to Santiago,
Chile, where he arrived on April 20, 194l. At that time
as well, TAKANO was employed by the Mitsui interest, inasmuch
as his United States address was given as Mitsui and Company,
New York City.

Dr. JUAN ESTEBAN MONTERO, P. 0. Box 2526 has been
identified as the 1932 President of Chile. Montero resides
and maintains his offices at Calle Santo Domingo 1382/6,
Santiago, Chile. He was born February 12, 1879, a Chilean
citizen, and has held prominent positions ranging from a
professorship of Roman law at the University of Chile, Minister
of Interior, up to the Presidency of Chile. He was elected
President of Chile for the constitutional period extending
from 1931 through 1937, but served only one year of this term
because of a revolutionary movement. Although it is known
that MONTFRO is a member of the Chilean hadical Party, which is
quite pro-democratic in its sentiments, MONP!tO has stated that
he believes the Germans will ultimately win the war.
At the present time it is known that MONTEhO is actively

Chilena Oriental, in an effort to liberate NAKATSUA and NAKAYAMA
from their internment as Japanese Nationals, which has been
ordered by the Chilean government.

Subject: Japanese Activities in Argentina
894.20210/223
As of possible interest to you, information is being furnished
concerning Japanese activities in Argentina. This information was
received from a reliable, confidential source and has been made available
to the American Embassy in Buenos Aires, Argentina.
It has been reported that Carlos Sapta Cruz, who is a Chilean,
is planning a trip for the Japanese to Bolivia, Peru, and Ecuador under
the direction of the Japanese Embassy in Buenos Aires. It is reported
that the purpose of this trip is to observe strategic areas, especially
oil bases, and the trip will last about two months. It is further reported
that Cruz is taking letters to Peru for the Japanese, which letters will
be hidden among his luggage. Reportedly the subject receives letters
at his address of Piedras 338, Buenos Aires, from his mother Luz Poblete
de Santa Cruz in Santiago, which letters are intended for the Japanese
in Buenos Aires.
This information has been made available to the American
Embassy in Buenos Aires, Argentina.

The question of foreign hostile interests being comnected
with the Compenia Minero Fetrolere Del Pacifico has arisen based upon
reports that this com any has employed in the past Japanese technicians
and has been reputed to be partly fimmnced by Japenese capital. It
should be noted that the Commercial Division of the United States Em-
bassy et uito, Ecuador, has revoked permission for the sale of the
Compania Minero Petrolera Del Pacifico to the International Petroleum
Company. The Stendard O1l Compeny of California 1s now reliably re-
ported to be attemptine to purchase the conceesion, end before the ap-
proval of the transaction will be given by the Commercial Division,
the question of foreign interests in the Company is egmin being mede
the subject of investigation. This is to determine whether a Treasury
License 1s needed in comyliance with Executive Order No. 8389, which
provides for cases of traneactions in which foreign hostile funde are
involved.
In connection with the propesed sale to the Standard Cil
Company of California and related matters. the questioning of the of-
ficere of the Company, Luis Martines Delgado and Gustavo Dies. Delgado,

of $80.000 (U.S.) in Colombia through 1nfluential friends in that country.
They stated that this amount of money was not sufficient to hire European
or American geologists and that sometime prior to Jume, l939, upon a
visit to Ecusdor of the Japanese Minister to Peru, he made a statoment
to the press that Japanese techniciens were ready and available for
work in Scuedor, thus influencing the officials of the Company to con-
tact the Japanese Minister with * view to obtaining techniciams for
explorstory work on the concession at a cheaper rate then white tech-

further stated that the matter was discussed with the Reuadoresn govern-

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
This is one of a series of reports on the Far East prepared for
the use of government Far East specialists by the Analysis Division
of the Foreign Broadcast Intelligence Service. Each report deals
more or less intensively with certain regions in the Far East, but
not all reports cover all regions. Briefer treatment of these and
other areas can be found in the regular Weekly Review issued by the
F.B.I.S. Communications should be addressed to the Chief Analyst,
1424 K Street, N.W., Washington, D.C. (Executive 3620, Extension
330.)
Readers will unäerstand that the material presented here is
necessarily fragmentary, and frequently monitored under unfavorable
reception conditions, so that accuracy with regard to places, names
and figures cannot always be guaranteed. The Japanese radios do not
broadcest regular, progressive reports on specific developments in
the Far East; rather they carry certain news items when those will
serve some useful propaganda purpose. It should also be borne in
mind that the material from Japanese and Japanese-controlled stations
is frequently mothing more than propaganda. Experts in the field
will be able to determino the accuracy or inaccuracy of Japanese
statements, in most cases, and will be able to supplement their own
sources of information with useful items of this report.

Home Front Control: In the Cabinet meeting of June 1 the govern-
ment decided to abolish or suspend public functions which have "no
direct connection with increasing fighting strength" (home service.
July 5). Hoshino. chief secretary of the Cabinet, proposed e simplifi-
cation of functions whereby decisions will be mae on methods for hold-
ing functions sponsored hereafter by any Ministry or non-government
organization only after consultation and approval by the Board of In-
formation. All the vice-ministers greed. Presumably there have been
so mony meetings and public gatherings on this and that that the
public's energies have beem to some extent diverted from activities
directly contributing to the war. The F.B.I.S. Japanese-language
staff sees in the restrictions a determination to narrow down such
activities, partly perhaps to free the public transportation systems from
congestion, or to heve a safeguard against things being said or done
by government or private groups that are inconsistent with the aims of
the government.

Flood of Goods: Says Tokvo English Domei July 16, "While the United
States and Britain are suffering from an acuto shortage of cardinal ar
materials Nippon is presently being flooded with important resources
flowing in from the southern regions.
Tin, one of the most important materials required by the war indus-
tries of Kippon is now allegedly supplied pbundantly from the southern
r gions which produce over 70 percent of the total world output. The
importation in "enormous quantity" of this vital material has resulted
in the reduction of the tin market price by 48 percent from the standard
price which was fixed by the government et 425 yen per 100 kilograms in
November of 194l. The present cut in tin prices which was officially
announced by the Ministry of Comerce and Industry was hailed by economic
circles as indicative of the "extremely sound wartime price structure"
of ippon as well cs the smooth progress of the development of natural
.res.ources.
Tokro adds in a German-language broadcest to Europe that the sane
is expected in regard to rubber, quinine, manila hemp and petroleum.
"With the progress of the work of cconomic development the price policy
of fighting Japan will tend more and more toward stabilizetion."

production was quadrupled. Two years ago there was an excess of produc-

statistics, but in 1938 and 1940 two special bills were passed deal ing
with measures supposed to foster home production. The industry was re-
organized and the number of, plants decreased from approximat ely 1,000
in 1940, to 400. It was one of the first branches of Japanese industry
to go under semi-government control.

granted by the stete, t he industry has improved so much "as far as machine
tool industry for armament purposes is concerned that Japan does not have

FREECHINA
In celebration of China's six years of resistance, Tokyo propagandists seize
the opportunity to loose another dart at their constant target in China:
"CEASE RESISTACE" (see Weekly Review, No. 84, July 1O, p. 13). . In Chinese
broadcasts from Tokyo, Japanese spokesmen are painfully confused as to why
Chungking continues to be the "puppet-traitor of China," the "turncoat of
G. E.A.," the "cat's-paw of the Anglo-Americans." "If Japan has committed
any blunders, she hopes Chungking will plainly advise her to refrain from
such action, " says Kamizo Tomogani in a Chinese commentary on July 8. He
hastily adds, "But I think Japan has made no blunder." Chungking is the
one, he says, who, through ignorance, has made the serious blunder of
mortgaging its country through Anglo-American promises of help which will
avail China nothing." Tokyo continually warns China to beware of Anglo-
Americans bribes and promises which are designed to keep China fighting
for them. Free China has become the "Orphan of Asia." This aspect of
China's international relations is reason enough for her to stop fighting;
but.added to this, as pictured by Tokyo, are conclusive evidences of her
internal disintegration.
CHINA AND THE UNITED STATES
Radio Chungking celebrates America's Independence Day by.paying tribute
to the spirit which sustained America through her long struggle for national
independence. Simultaneously, Tokyo propaganda concentrates on charges
against the United States, which to the Japanese mind, are convincing
proof that China dares not trust America as an Ally. In the general
pattern of discrediting the Anglo-Americans, Tokyo and other Axis radios
single out the United States for more specific attention during the past
fortnight. The chief culprit in perpetuating China's regrettable depend-
ence on America is Chiang Kai-shek, who has left "the command of China's
fighting services...at the disposal of Americans, the domestic administra-

at the beck and call of President.Roosevelt and.the United States." This
"traitorous" record disqualifies Chiang from having any right whatsoever
to speak for the Chinese people and their independence, as he. "unblushingly"
does in his recent work entitled China's Destiny.

the flat refusal of the Chungking demand, even at the sacrifice of the
unifioation of the Chinese races" (Tokyc, English, to Latin America,
7:30 a.n., July 13). In another broadcast on July. 16 Tokyo states, "Con-
fronted by a great political crisis the Rods are now holding an omergeney
(...) under the chairmanship of (Mao Chu Fan). In addition the Communist
officials of the Provincial governnent (...) are ordering the Communist
General Governnent to take drastic measures against the attitude being taken

Chungking Counteraction:  On July 17 Saigon reported in English to
Australia and New Zealand with a Nanking dateline: "Under instruction
from the Chungking Supreme National Defense Council. Chungking military
leaders in the northwestern provinces held a conference on July 10 and 11
under the chairmanship of Chen Cheng, Governor of Shansi Province, to,
discuss concrete military measures for dealing with Chinese Communists.
(Note: Latest official listing of Free China Provincial Governors place
Chen Cheng in Hupeh and Yen Hsi-shan in Shansi.) Those who attended the
meeting besides Chen Cheng included Hu Tsung-nan, Commander-in-Chief of the
Chungking 34th Group Army; Sun Wei-chu, Commander-in-Chief of the Fourth
Group; (Ting Pao-chun) Commander-in-Chief of the Shansi-Suiyuan Border
Zone; (Tao Kwan-ching) Commander of the 32nd Army; (Yang Shen) Vice-commander
of the Second War Zone, and Sun Ying-ting, Chief of Staff of the First War
Zone. At the conclusion of the conference, both Sun Wei-chu and (Ting
Pao-chun) proceeded to Chungking in order to discuss the concrete measures
to be taken against the Communists."
The pattern of the above Japanese report on a Chungking-Communist
crisis is interesting in the extent to which it parallels other Japanese
reports of similar "crisis" in the past. "Demands" and "counter-demands,"
"aggressive policies," "splits," "extraordinary sessions," "radical
elements, ". "purges," "suppressions,"--all appear in faithful succession
until finally comes a deadlock with the "breach" fatally widened between
the two groups. In August 1942 the initiative was said to have been taken
by the Communists in prosenting demands to Chungking, which responded
with a "categorical refusal" and "counter-demands." In August, 1942
Tokyo reported a "split" in the Communist Party, resulting in the explusion
of Chou En-lai as Communist Party representative in Chungking. The group
in the Party was said to be composed of followers of Mao Tse-tung, and the
other to be the "elders' faction." In September 1942, Tokyo reported that
"thc Communist Party has organized a purge corps ordered by Mao Tse-tung.
who is callad the Communist Dictator of China, to stamp out the Chungking
elements which have been disturbing the Red Party from within." An"extra-
ordinary" session of Communist Party leadors was called to deal with the
"crisis" then, as now.
Two slight variations are given by Tokyo in interpreting the present
situation, (1) The Communists are represented es prepared to give a
"flat refusal" to Chungking's demands, even at the expense of the "common
defense agreement." Tokyo radio material has not before made reference
to the Communists! willingness to abandon the United Front. (2) Tokyo
credits the Communists with obstructing a mounting "racial awakening" among
the people of the Chinese hinterlands. The realization is growing among
the Chinese people, says Tokyo, that Japan's "new policy" is much more pro-
ductive than Chungking's "negative" policy, The only "barrier" which pre-
venting all-out cooperation be ween Chungking and Japan is "the constant
attempt by the Chinese Communists to nip such a popular reawakening in
the bud" (Tokyo, English Domei, 11:00 a.m., July 8). In another broadcast
attacking Chiang Kai-shek, Tokyo declares, "There is left in Chungking not
even the slightest sign of the Three Peoples' Principles. As far as the
Chungking regime is concerned the Three Peoples' Principles have already
been replaced by Communism through compromise and dealings with the
Communist Party."

OCCUPIED CHINA
Japan seems to be marking time in her propaganda, spreading out her activ-
ities in China so that the impact of them may be felt without ever really
accomplishing anything crucial. The next step seems to be the formal
"return" of the International Settlement at Shanghai to the puppet Chinese,
scheduled for August 1. The anniversary of the opening of the "China
Incident" was not after all, the occasion of any new action--only speeches
and celebrations merked the occasion.
TREATY REVISION
There has been no further mention of the promised forthcoming re-
vision of the treaty between Japan and Nanking China. No explanation for
the delay or the silence 1s given by Tokyo. It hardly seems probable that
the revision was made but kept quiet since any "revision" would be mainly
for its effect on public opinion in Occupied and Free China.
INTERNATIONAL SETTLEMENT
Tokyo seems to be marking time until August 1 when the International
Settlement will be formally handed over to the puppets. However, it would
seem that conferences are continuing on the details of the agreement. On
July 10, Domei announced that a conference was held in Shanghai between
Japanese officials and the puppet Chinese of Shanghai "to discuss the handl-
ing of business of the SMC (Shanghai Municipal Council) following the
restoration of the International Settlement." "It is understood the
utilization of the Council's funds, payments of salaries of former officials
of the SMC and the disposition of leading personnel were the principal
topics of discussion."
EXTRATERRITORIALITY
There has been no announced agreement on the question of extraterritori-
ality. Radio materials point to various conferences and say that the
negotiations were conducted in the spirit of "mutual understanding" and
that satisfactory progress was made (Transocean, July 3). Dome1 on the
same date was a little more specific in saying that "an amicable settlement
was reached regarding the question of the right of taxation." It is
problematic whether any agreement reached will be announced before August 1
when the International Settlement will be formally handed over--lest an
extraterritorial agreement detract too much from the deed at hand.
FOREIGN CONCESSIONS
French Concession at Shanghai: Predictions still continue that the
French Concession will be "returned" to the puppet administrators very
soon. The formal agreement ias not yet been announced as signed.
Italian Concession at Tientsin: Berlin reports that a 20 year agree-
ment concerning the Itelian Concession in Tientsin was reached. It was

CENTRAL ECONOMIC
Three Yeer Plan: According to MAINICHI dispatch, the three year plan
is "large-scale" and seems to be mostly devoted to waterworks and communioa-
tions (Domei, July 9).
Under a three year plan, the first year will be devoted to repairing
and reinforcement of the embankment along the Grand Canal while in the
second year construction of large embankments along the Wei River and also
around the Hungtze Lake in Kiangsu province will be undertaken. Reinforce-
ment of the Yangtze embankment will be launched in the third year. Upon
completion of the project the water level of Hungtze lake will be elevated,
recleiming approximately seven million acres of land. A 279-kilometer new
canal will be constructed in northern Kiangsu province and 7,O0o square
kilometers of land will be converted into cotton producing areas. The cost
of this project will be 300 million yuan. Swamp lands are to be reclaimed
on the eastern bank of Lake Tai in Kiangsi province to create 45, O00 acres
of arablc land. A waterway will be dredged from Lake Tai to Tienshan
lake and there will be cmbankments "to free the region from the annual
floods which have heretofore swept the area. The Wi river project will
have locks.
Some of the embankments have been destroyed "by retreating Chungking
troops," claims Tokyo.
Roads: Some contemplated highways are: the Nanking-Shanghai road

Pengpu in Anhwei province "having a military as well as a political value";
