Wang Keh-min was born in Chekiang province and first went into the
diplomatic service when he was appointed the advisor to a group of Chinese
students studying in Japan. Later he was the counselor of the Embassy in
Tokyo. After serving in France, he was appointed the President of the
Bank of China and served as "Home Minister from 1917 to 1918 in the Wang
Tsu-liu cabinet." He was a member of the Hopei-Chahar Political Council
and assumed the chairmanship of the North China puppets after the outbreak
of "the China Incident." Upon the advent of the "National Government" at
Nanking, when the name of the North China puppet group was changed he
was still the chairman until June 1939 when "he resigned in favor of Wang
I-tang." It seems that he has been the heed of the North China "advisory
council" to the puppet council since. Recently he has been in "Manchukuo"
and in Nanking a short time ago (Domei in English, July 4).
He is now appointed to the chairmanship of the Hsin Min Hui and in
an "instructive address" pointed out "the importance of the Hsin Min
Society as the leading organization of the people of North China." The
official investiture ceremony was to be held on July 20 (Japanese domestic
broadcast, July 5).
On July 12 he left for Nanking by plane "to r port his assumption of
office to President Wang Ching-wei" announced the North China :information
bureau. Wang Shih-ching will be acting chairman in Wang Keh-min's absence.
A Japanese domestic broadcast on July 5 says with a Hsinking dateline
that Wang Keh-min is "expected...(to) devote most of his effort on the
promotion of the political and economic collaboration between Manchukuo
and North China." There has been no monitored comment from the Central
puppets on his assumption of the new post even though he is now in Nanking.

Rice Shortage: Chungking reports a severe rice shortage in the
Shanghai area. The rice shops are now selling the worst grade of rice
to the public. The rice sold is that usually given to chicken, declares

are reported mixed in with it. At first, the rice shops were supplied
with rice from the warehouses four times a month, but later it was changed
to three times with the amount of each delivery substantially decreased
in volume. Prices are also going up (Chungking in Cantonese to North
America, July 6).
The food situation is getting so bad that "the courts find themselves

farmers attempting to take rice into Shanghai have been shot by Japanese

The visit of Premier Tojo and plans for the anti-esponiage week held July
12 to 18 receive the major share of attention on Far East radios during

POLITICAL DEVELOPMENTS
The Tojo Visit: The Premier arrived in Batavia in the morning of
July 7 where he was welcomed by "Supreme Comnander Harada and a lar e
number of locel military leaders, government officials and civilians and...
the joyful cheers of the crowds of native inhabitants who were lined along
the road" (Tokyo, home service, July 12). Following a conference with
Commander Harada on military and administrative matters, Tojo,attended a
rally of 100,O00 Indonesians held at the Gunbil plaza in Batavia. In
addressing the assembled crowd Tojo reiterated his "imperishable conviction
of certain victory" for Japan, described his "happiness" in visiting the
southern regions and witnessing for himself the cooperation extended by the
natives of these areas (Tokyo, home service, July l2). Ir Soekarno, chair-
man of "Poetera," also spoke at the rally and, as the representative of
the entire 50 million Javanese, pledged "the strong resolve...for positively
cooperating with Hippon" (Tokyo. English Domei, July 12). Similar senti-
ments of happiness and pledges of cooperation were expressed by four
native rajas--of Solo, Mangkoe, Negoro, Djokjo and Paloealaman--after on
interview with the Premier.
Anti-Esptanage Week: July 12 to 18 was set nside as a period of
emphasis on counter-espionage work in the Celebes. Elaborate schedules of
activities were announced for each dey. These activities consisted largely
of speeches by native and Japanese leaders, the singing of songs by students
and dropping of pamphlets in Makassar. As reported by Makassar to Indonesia
on July 6 the program was to be as follows: On the first day employees of
"Naishoko) "will go around in cars with pamphlets concerning how to disable
spies in their work with Makassar as the area." Other organizetions were to
distribute similar pamphlets. Propaganda material in speeches and pamphlets
was to be distributed on the second day by students and employees of speci-
fied firms who were stationed throughout the city. The third day's program
consisted of motion pictures "about the rising sun and the GA war which
will help fight the spies." Speeches, each 70 minutes long, were to be
given by two officials of the Japanese military administration. Programs
for the subsequent days followed the same pattern with the inclusien of
songs by students. But more pamphlets were dropped, pictures shown and long
speeches given all of which showed the necessity of discounting "whisper
ing campaigns and false rumors" and of destroying the spie "knorm to be
·living in the city."
Governors' Conference: Instructions given to Harada by Tojo filtered
down to provincial governors at a conference held July 12 in Batavia. The
meeting was attended by high military and civil officials, chiefs of all
bureaus and departments and governors of all provinges. This wns the second

INDONESIAN ECONOMY
Resources: Development of mining, agricultural and marine resources
of the Celebes area is to be the function of the scientific institute
opened July 7 in Makassar says Tokyo in a French broadcast to Indo-China
on July 7. The institute, which will be headed by e Dr. Ichiro Tanabe,
will also "re-edit" the knowledge of scientists who have done previous
research in the area. Dr. Tanabe is quoted in a domestic broadcast from
Tokyo on July 16 in which he statesthat by bringing superior Japanese
techniques to the southern regions, Japen will be able to develop the
abundant material resources of the area, "those which the Dutch could not
develop."
A shortage of asphalt and a surplus of rubber will both be eliminated
by the extensive use of a newly developed paving material made of rubber
says Tokyo in an English Domei dispatch on July ll. The process was dis-
covered by the East Coast Agriculture Experimental station of Sumatra.
"The paving material is manufactured by first heating a mixture of palm
oil and lime at 150 degrees Fahrenheit, adding chemically treated rubber
scrap and again employing heat for two hours. The solution then is poured
out over a rock bed end allowed to cool, forming a durable pavement."
Tests have proved that the new substance is equal to, and even superior
in some instances to, asphalt according to the report, especially in wear-
ing and "adhesive qualities."
A substitute for previously imported woolen cloth has been discovered
at the Ched Bogol Agricultural Experimental Station near Batavia says Tokyo
in an English Domei dispatch of July 8. The substitute fabric which is
reported to be superior to pure woolen goods in its wearing, washing and
dyeing qualities is produced by the "Attucus Ricini" specie of silkworm
which feeds on castor bean leaves. The silkworm works so rapidly that the
cocoon may be unwound 15 days after it is started. Raising.of the worms
on an industrial scale will be begun "immediately."
Button demands of the entire GEA will be supplied when arrangements
are completed for large scale collection of sea shells in the Makassar
region (Tokyo, English Domei, July 6). "According to present plans the
shells will not be shipped to Japan as hitherto but roughly shaped into
buttons on the spot and then finished in Japan."
”、
Production and sale of salt in west Borneo is to be the function of
the newly organized Salt Production Sales Association (Tokyo, English Domei,
July l4). Present efficiency in production surpasses that of former times
but will be still further improved by the use of Japanese technigues in the
salt fields at Singkawang. "Various other measures have also. been taken to
encourage production including tex exemption for salt, enforcement of
officiel salt prices and granting subsidies to salt producers." Emphasis
is also being placed on salt production in the Celebes where a new salt
field has been partially completed near Makassar. The product of this new
field when combined with that of previously operated fields will be suf-
ficient to meet the demands of the entire southern regions (Singapore,
Domei July 12).

Finance: Drastic price control measures enforced by the military

SOCIAL PROBLEMS
Health: Malaria is now "raging" in the central Celebes says Tokyo
in a home service broadcast on June 28. The month of July has been set
aside for a campaign to eliminate the fever. Mosquito breeding areas are
being destroyed and malaria patients treated. In addition a Tropical
Disease Research Institute will be established in Makassar under direction
of Dr. Oshiro, graduate of the Tohoku Imperial University medical depart-
ment and "well known authority on dengue fever" (Tokyo, English Domei,
July 8).
Use of vitamin C in the treatment of diabetes is reported by Tokyo in
an English Domei dispatch of July l4. This trcatment was discovered by an
Indonesian chemist, Soctarman, who also perfected a method for manufactur-
ing a romedy for diabetes containing the vitamin.
Morning calisthenics, a traditional feature of Japanese home trans-
missions will now be broadcast from Makassar according to a June 30 broad-
cast from that station. Indonesians are urged to gather in groups to re-
ceive the greatest benefit from the excrcises. This is presumably part of
the campaign to reduce the prevalence of tuberculosis which was reported
to be extensive in the Celebes last spring.

THE
PHILIPPINEISLANDS
Premier Tojo's second visit to the Philippines allegedly reflects the "secure
and enviable position" which the Filipino people occupy in his heart and
in the hearts of the Japanese, and is said to have brought "fresh encour-
agement" to 18 million Filipinos who are suddenly brought face to face
with "full independence" (Domei, July 12).
Confidence is further tolstered at this hour of supreme challenge by
the dramatic rise of the Preparatory Committee for Philippines Independence
which has occupied the center of the Philippines stage for the past month.
This body is actually engaged in drafting a constitution "that fits the
time in which we live" (Camilo Osias Manila, June 24). However, its title
and dramatization suggest that it is intended to solve all problems
connected with independence. Thus all.the organs of the Executive Com-
mission have been "placed at its disposal" by Executive Order of June 22
and Premier Tojo himself increased the prestige of the new architects of
Philippines independence by calling in person upon the Committee and com-
mencing its progress (Tokyo, July 12).
Jose P. Laurel, Commissioner of the Interior and President of the
Independence Committee, expressed the hope that it will succeed in evolving
a plan of government for the New Philippines "that will reflect popular
will and advance the interests of the nation and also meet the many difficult
problems that will confront our new nation as an intogral part of the Co-
prosperity Sphere of Greater East Asia" (Manila, June 30). He further
asserted that the fundamental policy of the Committee in laying the founda-
tions for Philippine's independence will be to secure the well-being of the
Filipinos (Tokyo, July 6).
Meanwhile the real arbiters of Philippines fate have temporarily with-
drawn from the limelight. A single brief dispatch reveals that the military
administration held a two-day confercnce of.its various branch chiefs to
discuss "vital problems" accompanying the beginning of preparations for
Philippine independence and "various measures" related to the strengthening
of the military administration (Domei, July 13, monitored by New Delhi).
POLITICAL
Committee for the Preparation of Philippines Independence: Instructions
for the creation of this body were issued direct from Tokyo to Lieut.
General Kuroda who passed them on to tho Kalibap1 National Convention,
then in scssion in Manila. The business of selecting 20 persons as "repre-
sentativos of the people of the Philippines" progressed smoothly and
"without a hitch." A nominating committee of five persons was unanimously
chosen by the convention on. June 18. The following day at the giant rally
hold in Luneta Park in gratitude for Premier Tojo's latest promise of
independencc, the names of the mombors of the Independonce Committee were
announced by Benigno Aquino, Director General of the Kalibapi, and allegedly
received the "adherence" of the multitude. In broadoasting the ontire pro-
coodings of the rally to the Japanese Broadoesting Corporation in Tokyo,
the announcer twiee remarked, "The announcement of these names 1: forbidden
yet, so please bear this in mind."

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.99)
-F2 -
On June 20 the Japanese expeditionary forces in the Philippines
announced that the members of the Committee had been formally appointed by
Lieut. General Shigenori Kuroda upon the unanimous recommendation of the
Kalibapi (Domei, June 20). Jose Laurel, Commissioner of the Interior, heads
the Committee, while the first Vice-President is Ramon Avancena, former
Chief Justice and at present a member of the Council of State and Superior
Judge of the Philippines High Court. Benigno Aquino, Director General of
the Kalibapi has been named second Vice-President. The other members of
the Committee are: Jorge B. Vargas, Chairman of the Executive Commission;
Antonio De Las Alas, Commissioner of Finance; Teofilo R. Sison, Commissioner
of Justice; Rafael Alunan, Commissioner of Agriculture; Claro M. Recto,
Commissioner of Health and Public Welfare; Quintin Paredes, Commissioner
of Public Works andCommunications; Jose Yulo, Chief Justice of the Supreme
Court, General Emilio Aguinaldo; Miguel Unson, member of the Council of
State; Camilo Osias, Assistant Director General of the Kalibapi; Vicente
Madrigal, Member of the Council of State; Manuel C. Briones; Emiliano
Tirona, Director of the Bureau of Employment; Manuel Roxas; Pedro Sabido,
Manager of the Abaca Corporation; Sultan Alaoya Alsonto Saramain; and
Melicio Arranz, former Senator.
These twenty men "represent" all the important linguistic groups and
the three regional divisions of the Philippines--Luzan, Visaya and Mindanao.
Mssrs. Laurel, Recto, and Roxas were among those who drafted the Common-
wealth Constitution of 1935, a fact which is said to prove that the past
political experience of the Filipino people will be drawn upon in drafting
the new constitution. However, the presence in the Committee of Vargas,
Laurel, and Aquino is said to "guarantee" that the experience of the past
will be "suitably adapted" to the necds of the New Order (Domei, July 12).
(Arsenio) Sebastian, judge of the court of the first instance, has been
selected as the chief secretary of the Commission (Manila, June 23).
The members of the Independence Committee unanimously voted for the
organization of six sub-committeas and agreed to leave the selection of their
chairmen and members to tho discretion of Commissioner Laurel (Domei,
June 30). The Constitution Drafting Committee will be headed by Commissioner
Laurel himself and will be responsible for the actual drafting of the
constitution, incorporating therein the recommendations of the other
committees. It is noteworthy that its members include all three of the
men who aro expected to see that past experience is suitably adapted to
the requirements of the New Order.
The six different committees with their rospective chairmen and members
are the following!
Administrative Department Committees: Commissioner Rafael R. Alunan,
Chairman; Magistrate Manuel C. Briones, Manuel Roxas, Suftan Saramain,
Pedro Sabido.
n

Rafael Alunan, Camilo Osias, General Emilio Aguinaldo, Vincente Madrigal.
Committee of the Judicial Department: Commissioner Teofilo Sison,
Chairman; Hagistrate Briones, (First Name) (Pirson), Pedro Sabido, and
Commigsioner Antonio De Ies Alas.

Popular Support: "The people owe it to thenselves," urges Canilo
Osias, "to give those public servants of theirs their completo support
and cooperation.... This is no tine to wish (for) what night have been;
this is no time to indulge in dreaming.... These are truly tines for
creative living" (Manila, June 22).
There are indications that the creation of the Independence Connittee
has given risen to considerable discussion of a nture disturbing to the
authorities. Leon Quinto, Mayor of Manila, urges the people not to
"waste their time in idle talk and gossip" and to avoid "unnecessary
disturbances" and adds that if there are any good suggestions with regard
to the present national issue, he is sure the Committee will be willing
to consider them (Manila, July 13). The Mayor also "allayed all fears"
aroused by the "false rumor" that conscription will be the price of
Philippine independence, citing the Director General of the military admini-
stration to the effect that Japan has no need for soldiers from the countries
under her influence, since "Japan has at least 25,000,000 men prepared and
trained to take up arms whenever the Government (thinks it necessery)."
Mr. Quinto concluded with an appeal to "all the guerrillas who still infest
the mountains" and to those who form "a militant insignificant minority"
to cease their efforts to disrupt "this fruitful period of our policital
life" (Manila, July 6).
At about the same time, Hilarion S. Silayan, Director of the Bureau
of Plant Industry, who is being royally wined and dined and otherwise im-
pressed together with other members of the Philippines inspeotion party
which is touring Japan, declares optimistically, "We shall find no dif-
ficulty in our task of convincing our people of the principle of Hakko
Ichiu, because all the past acts of Japan are living proof of her noble
intention" (Dome, July 1).
Nonetheless, Japan's spokesmen in the Philippines are constantly point-
ing to new proofs of Japan's sincerity, while independence in all its glory
and imminence and hallowed by countless generations of martyrs who have
fought and died for it is their favorite theme. The universality of this
appeal is not marred by specific definition of its "abundant blessings" or
of the "obligations" which it entails. There is even a marked decrease
in references to the pre-requisites for independence originally stated by

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.98)
- F4 -
the Direotor General of the military administration, namely. peace and
order, economic self-sufficienoy, and a return to the spirit of Asia.
These requirements are now subordinatod to a single plea, that for unity.
For the most part the Filipinos are being urged simply to "unite"
under the banner of independence. and speakers claim that tho Filipinos
have boen asked to do "little enough" for independenoe, simply to make
thomselves a strong united nation.
According to Benigno Aquino, Director Genoral of the Kalibapi,"...
independence is the only standard beneath which the Wilipino people have
been solidly and unalterably united." However, indications abound that
thoy are not so united by the prosent previow of independonce. Thus Mr.
Aquino adds that the Kalibapi will strive "as far as it is humanly possible"
to marshal into action "tho lethargic olements of the country" sinee it
realizes that this is no time "to hesitate, to doubt, or to waver."
The xistence of doubts and hesitation is further indicated by
Frencisco Icasiano of the Tribune editorial staff who writes on the birth-
day of Jose Rizal, "Rizal would nover ask, 'What independence will they
give us?' Rather he would say, 'Let us put all our might and determination
into the perfoction of this gift whose coming we've awaited for over four
hundred years.'" Tho martyr, ho adds, would never have hoeded "silly pro-
paganda and chatter...about freedom," "The kind of indepondence we'll
got," according to Mr. Icasiano, "1s the kind we desorvo" (Dome1, June 19).
That unity is still to be achieved is further suggested by the Honor-
ablo Melicio Arranz, former Sonator, who remarks that unity requires that
"we firmly rosolve to adjust completely our social and moral standard
(and) our mode of living, and observe a strong national discipline" (Manila,
June 25). The Japanese pattern of this much-streesed "unity" is also re-
vealed by Camilo Osias, Secretary Genoral of the Kalibapi, who declares
that the Junior Kolibapi is intended to produce "like-minded men" (Manila,
June 19).
A certain dissatisfaction with the results of the impact of the promises
of independence on the Filipino people is suggested by the newspapor ASAHI
which expressed the "confidence" that when the joy over Philippine indepen-
dence is "orystallized" the greater obstacles can be overcome (Manila,
June 28).
General Aguinaldo is equally optimistic declaring, "As an independent
people, we oan with happier heart oooperate much better with the Japanese
cmpire" (Manila, June 17). The General sees all troublesome doubts dis-
polled by the light of Japan's rising sun by whose.rays "we-have found the
contral fact of our national existonce; tho eternal truth whioh cluded us,
but to whioh our national soul was anohored from the boginning, namely that
we are Oriontals and that it is our God-givon duty to do our part as members
of that proud race" (Domei, July 13).
Constabulary: Two hundred new polioemen are to bo added to the
metropolitan constabulary of Manila with a corresponding numbor of officors.

This increase on the police force is considered "necessary" in view of
its manifold activities which include enforcing the rules and regulations
issued by the administration for the improvement of economic conditions
within the city (Manila, June 25).
Legal Reforms: Recommendations of the last convention of judges and
prosecutors envisaging amendments of existing laws and regulations are
being reviewed and elaborated by the Department of Justice (Manila, June 25).
Neigiborhood Associations: A general convention of officers and
members of the district and neighborhood associations was called by Mayor
Quinto on July 4. The assemblage was treated to the impressions of the
Mayor and Pio Duran, Assistant Director General of the Kalibapi, gathered
during their recent inspection tour of Japan. (The Mayor and Mr. Quinto
returned suddenly to Manila by plane leaving the rest of the inspection
party in Japan.)
Moros: In order to attain unity and understanding between the leaders
and peoples of the Moro tribe, a "consultative council" headed by Sultan
Saramain was recently formed. The Sultan is a member of the Independence
Preparatory Committee, and two Moro youths are among the Filipinos who
were recently selected to study in Japan. A number of Japanese language
schools are being established in Zamboanga, Cotabato and the Sulu States
(Domei, July 1).
Peace and Order: Economic as well as political independence of the
Philippines is dependent upon complete restoration of peace and order
throughout the islands, according to the Tokyo and Manila radios which are
assisting the military administration in the increasingly intensified
campaign being carried on to destroy resistance. Problems of peace and
order were discussed at the Kalibap1 conference in June and at a sub-
sequent conference of provincial governors and mayors. On both occasions
the audiences were deluged with both orders and requests for complete
destruction of belligerent individuals and groups so that the people may
resume their normal economic activities as well as be prepared for the
much longed for grant of independence.
An indication of the fact that the campaign is by no means near
completion, despite radio claims that complete peace and order has been
restored, is the creation of a new bureau in the Commission of the Interior
to be known as the Bureau of Information and Public Security. Manila
reports in a Spanish broadcast on July 1C that Chairman Vargas, with,
of course, approval of the supreme commander of the Japanese forces in
the islands, promulgated Executive Order 173 which creates the new unit.
"The Bureau of Information and Public Security, which will have juris-
diction over all provinces and cities of the Philippines will exercise
the following functions: To circulate proper and correct information and
to develop a more intelligent and enlightened public opinion about the new
order, with a view toward achieving the quick re-establishment of peace
and order all over the Philippines; to study and exploit ways and means
which will be useful in promoting the activities of the Central Pacifica-
tion Committee with special reference to the urgent need for immediately

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
-F6-
re-establishing'complete peace and order; to (obtain) and submit such
reports (from) the public as the Commissioner of the Interior may order
from time to time, as circumstances may require; and to discharge various
othr duties pertaining to investigation and information on pacification
and public security."
The little active resistance remaining is the result of ignorance on
the part of isolated people, according to Manila. An official who
addressed the Kalibapi conference was quoted by Manila on June 21: 90 per
cent of the Filipino people have complied with the conditions set and are
ready for independence.  "Those people who have not yet complied are those
who cannot be reached by radio, newspaper or by any other means.... Those
who have been reached through the radio and papers are convinced of the
true (spirit) of (Japan)."
Beoause of this state of ignorance, mere pacification is not enough.
Camilo Osias, assistant director general of the Kalibapi, addressed
students of the Philippines Constabulary Academy on the necessity for
preventive and educational work among the few remaining belligerents
(Manila in English, July 9). Similar references are to be found in other
broadcasts.
Meanwhile Manila reports resistance in the following areas from time
to time: Negros, Nueva Ecija, Luguna, Ilocos Sur, Batangas, Sorsogon,
northern Cebu island and the hinterland of Davao.
The progress of the pacification campaign is indicated by the following
reports. General Ricarte, on a pacification tour of the Bicol Provinces,
conferred with the Governors of Rizal and Laguna and the Mayor of San Pablo.
All of them assured him that the protectors of public order are fulfilling
their duties and that the pacification carpaign is progressive rapidly.
"The only problem that remains is the (intransigence) of certain rebellious
elements in the mountains" (Manila, July 3).
Rafael Alunan, Commissioner of Agriculture and Commerce, has submitted
his written report to the Director General of the military administration.
on his recent pacification trip to Negros and other provinces in the
Visayan Islands. In general, the Commissioner reported that the situation
in the Visayas has "greatly improved" and that peace and order have been
restored "in many parts" (Manila, July 9).
On June 26 Manila remarked that the pacification campaign in the Central
Luzon Provinces have been enjoying great success as proved by the surrender
of a guerrilla leader in Nueva Ecija. However, a subsequent report from
Zaragosa. Nueva Ecija reveals that the municipal committee in charge of
pacification has been reorganized (Manila, June 1l).
ECONOMIC
The establishment of "self-sufficient" economic structure is, all
speakers agree, of the greatest importance, How ver, all display a distinct
tendency to slide over this aspect of the preparation for independence.

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
The people are urged to work for self-sufficient, but its attainment is no
longer said to be a necessary pre-requisite for the grant of independence.
Claims of economic progress are unusually vague and few in number.
Interviewed by the press together with the other members of the Executive
Commission on June 19, Arsenio Bonifacio, acting Commissioner of the
Interior, declared that with the help of the Department of Agricultural
and Commerce, the people's "vital needs" are being supplied, contributing
to "overall contentment and tranquility." Jose San Victories, Assistant
Commissioner of Agriculture and Commerce, merely referred to "plans"
directed toward the attainment of self-surficiency and the "harmonization"
of economic development in the Philippines with that of Greater East Asia.
Antonio De Las Alas, Commissioner of Finance referred to "tentative plans"
now being made to secure a sound financial basis for an independent
Philippines including the establishment of a central bank to refinance
local banking institutions (Domei, June 20).
Financo: In a fuller version of this last statement, Domei reports on
Juno 23 that Mr. De Las Alas stressed that a new central Filipino Bank
should have a strong financial basis, for which Japan's financial collabora-
tion in the form of the extension of credits is "vitally necessary." The
Commissioner expressed the hope that a greater number of Filipinos will
venture into the Islands' industrial and business fields with the attain-
ment of political independence, and declared that e central bank would
greatly oncourage and facilitate such participation. Present banking
facilities, he said, consist of the National Bank and three Japanese-owned
banks, the Yokohame Specio Bank, the Bn of Taiwan and the Southern
Development Bank with the most extensiv activities carried on by the
latter bank which advancos the funds for the industrial development of the
Islands, besides handling all treasury tusiness connected with the military
administration. The only function of the Netional Bank mentioned by
Mr. De Las Alas is the handling of treasury business connected with the
Executive Commission.
It appears, however, that the National Bank is being allowed to
expand its activities, since Domei reports on July 16 the rcopening of
three of its branches in the Cagayan Valley. Offices in Bayombong, Nueva
Vizcaya; Tuguagarao, Cagayan; and Ilegan, Isabela were opened on June 16,
23 and 26 respectively and will extend loans to farmers and merchants
to boost local agriculture and industries.
This extension of banking facilities was admittedly approved in
view of "the urgent need for extending aid to the farmers of the valley"
and the banks are expected to contribute to the increase of food produc-
tion in accordance with the five year plan of the military administration.
Domo1 reports from Manila on July 16 that Government revenue from
internal revenuo and customs taxes during the month of May showed an in-
crease of approximately 300,000 pesos over the previous month. Manila
remarks on July 10 that most of the municipal and provineial governments
in the Bicol provinces at presont have a groater income from existing
sources of revenue than before the war.

Food Production: Citing a review of food production during the nast:
year and a half, prepared by the Department of Agrieulture and Commerce,
Domei reports on July 17 that approximately 1,600,000 hectares of land
representing more than one-third of the total cultivated area in the"
Philippines has been planted in essential food crops such as rice, corn and
cassava. Truck farms on Luzon alone are said to number more than 68, 000.
The Preparatory Committee for Philippines Independence "notes with
satisfaction the self-sufficiency of food production already attained"
and is grateful for the "farsighted policy" of the military administration
in adopting the food production campaign, initiated one year ago, which
saved the people from "undue hardship" as far as staple crops were con-
cerned by distributing seeds and seedlings free of charge, furnishing
technical and agricultural assistance and various other measures.
Most "encouraging" aspect of the present program of agricultural
development. is, according to Domei, the fact that "there is every indica-
tion" that the Philippines will attain a food-production record surpassing
the prewar mark.
The administration of the food production intensification campaign
has received information indicating that the great majority of small
planters in the rice producing provinces are faced with lack of funds to
meet the expenses connected with sowing. Some officials connected with
the campaign would have the administration help these planters with small
loans in accordance with the needs of the applicants but not exceeding 50
pesos per hectare to be repaid immediately after the harvest, according
to Manila on June 26.
On July 8 Manila reports from Malolos, Bulacan, that hundreds of
farmers have been given seeds free by the Government, and that town mayors
and officials of the neighborhood associations have been instructed to
help in the promotion of agriculture.
Ways and means of obtaining increased food production and improving
the rice industry were discussed at a convention in- Manila of some 270
dolegates of the Municipal Rice Growers Cooperative Association, represent-
ing 13l municipal cooperatives in each of the rice-producing provinces of
Central Luzon (Manila, July 15). Other business on the agenda included
the organization of additional chapters of the National Rice Growers
Cooperative Association in Pangasinan and Tarlac which are said to be the
only two provinces where duly constituted provincial chapters heve not al-
ready been set up. The delegates will also receive instructions from the
proper authorities concerning the cooperation which the administration
expects from the municipal producers cooperative sociefes in the production
and distribution of grains in the Philippines (Manila, July 16). According
to Domei on July 17 (to the Americas) 92 rice growers' cooperativos and 107
agricultur:l cooperatives have been organized in connection with the food
production campaign to which they have greatly contributed by educating
farmers on the value of occupational teamwork besides facilitating super-
vision and control.

CULTURE
Education: Re-establishment of educational facilities in accordance
with the "new idea" of the Philippines is making rapid progress, says
Tokyo. A homo sorvice broadcast on July 2 reports that emphasis is being
placed on modicine, pharmacy, engineering and agriculture "which are
nocessery to the reconstruction of the Philippines." In addition to re-
opening of existing facilities in the above fiolds, schools have been
opened for the training of administrators and teachers. "Recently it was
docided to reopen the College of Pharmacy and the Home Economics college
of the Philippino Women's University and the Ophthalmology College of the
Sento Esperado Women's University."
In line with this "new" policy, two new schools, one for boys and the
second for girls, are to be opened in Cebu. "The courses in the new
schools (will) include Nippongo which will be taught one or two hours weekly
while English will be completely elimineted" (Manila in English to the
Philippines, July 12). Thesc are but two of the 600 elementary schools
scheduled to be opened this year, These schools will be staffed by the
840 Filipino teachors of Japanese who have completed training courses held
by thc Bureau of Public Instruction, according to a Domei dispatch in
English from Tokyo on June 24.
The so-called practioal courses were also emphasized in tho awarding
of scholarships to 92 students reported in an English Domei dispatch from
Tokyo on July 16. Tho scholarships were awarded to s tudents in engineering.
dontistry. pharmacy and in tho junior college of the University of the
Philippines.

Jepancse commender in the Islands, filled the vacancy left by the appoint-

their mutual combined efforts in national reconstruction."
A part of the program of propagation is an exhibit of historical

Health and Public Welfare. "Among the documents exhibited is a copy of
the first published in the Philippines edition of the Tagalog vocabulary
written by Franciscan Father Pedro De San Buenaventura in 16l3....
Another Filipino relic is a 1752 edition of a (...) book written by Father
Franciseo to give fellow Spaniards better facilities to learn Tagalog"

Propagation of Nippongo will be the function of a new'society to

M A L A Y A
The visit of Premier Tojo to the southern regions and particularly his
overnight stay in Singapore was the major social and political topic in
Radio Tokyo's references to Malaya during the fortnight.
POLITICAL DEVELOPMENTS
The Premier: Tojo's arrival was greeted by thousands of Malays,
Chinese, Japanese, Indonesians and Indians, says Tokyo (English Domei,
July 4) who waved flags and cheered when the Premier appeared in the
central plaza of Singapore. After this public appearance he conferred
with Field Marshal Count Terauchi, commander of all Japanese forces in the
southern area, and other military leaders. He then met with the sultans
of the Malay states all of whom according to Radio Tokyo, were "profoundly
moved" by the magnanimous spirit of Japan. Arising at 5 a.m. on the second
day of his visit, Tojo "mingled informally and intimately" with soldiers of
the Malayan army, and visited the war dead memorial, the Ford assenbly
plant at which the surrender of the Allies took place last year and the
army hospital (Tokyo in nglish to Latin America, July 6).
Conference: Governors of all Malayan provinces attended a conference
on administrative problems held July 12 at the military administration head-
quarters in Singapore, says Tokyo to the home audience on July 13. "There
was an instructional address on the policy regarding military administration
measures given by a military administration official and an address by
the chief of the General Affairs department." Each governor reported on
conditions in his province'with reference to "participation in political
affairs by the native inhabitants, the problem of foodstuffs production
increase, and the problem of the handling of rubber plantations which are
problems requiring immediate attention." For the benefit of the governors
a representative of the administration "clarified" the policies which will
be followed in solving these issues. These policies will be determined by
the plans outlined by Tojo in his June Diet speech and by discussions held
in Tokyo in which military administrators of the various regions participated
says Tokyo. The principal points to be followed, according to a French Domei
dispatch of July 3, are "first, exploitation of the resources necessary
for national defense and sending of these resources of Japan and second,
integral realization of regional economic self-sufficiency." In addition
to the problems mentioned above and to those of banking and finance, the
governors apparently discussed the questions involved in the transfer of
the four northern provinces to Thailand, for the governors were told to
"formulate appropriate measures until special instructions come from Japan in
connection with the transfer of the four states in the north to Thailand
and thereby see to it that there would be no change in administration."
ECONOMICS
Shortages: Inadequate supplies of clothing and medical equipment
"and other necessities" are being supplemented rapidly, says Tokyo. To
fill these needs heavy industries are being encouraged and domestic industry
by Chinese and Malays for production of "articles for daily use" is being
stressed (Domci in French, July 3).

The Higher Maritime Training Schools has been opened in Singapore to

In the future Malay seamen will be known by the Japanese names denoting

The reason for the closing, possibly lack of students, was not mentioned.

COMPROMISE OFFERS IN THE OFFING
Enemy sources claim that the arrival in Singapore of Bose and the
formation of the National Army have caused extreme consternation in the
councils of the Government of India. Saigon on July 12. quoting the
LONDON TIMES, reports that the main topic discussed at the Indian National
Defense Council. which was in session from July 3 to July 8. was how to
cope with the activities of fifth columnists and nationalists. Axis radios
warn repeatedly that the British answer to the new threat will be another
attempt at compromise.
From Istanbul, on July 7. Tokyo hears "the British-Indian government
feeling restless about internal conditions in India which are becoming more
serious is reported to be contemplating once again crafty methods of re-
conciliation with the Indian National factions, proposing that if these
factions should go back on their determination to oppose Britain the treat-
ment accorded now interned leaders would be, made lighter." A week later
"political observer in Tokyo suggested that the British Government attempted
to appease the all-Indian National Congress with the release of political
prisoners on the condition that the Congress Party withdraw its independence
demands."
Transocean reports a proposal of Sir J.P. Scrivastava that Congress
members who have been released from prison hold a rump meeting of the party
committee and cancel the demand for independence made at the Bombay meeting
last year in order to en le nationalists to resume talks to the British
authorities. Saigon speaks of a "feeler" put out on July 6 by the New
Delhi radio, in the form of an endorsement by Mr, Mnnwhi, former Congress
Minister in the Bombay government of the Srivastava plan. Munshi is critie-
ized sharply by both Saigon and the National Congress radio,
On July 13, Istanbul again 1s the source for a single unconfirmed report

THAILAND
Premier Pibul Songgram receives more than his usual share of attention
from Radios Tokyo and Bangkok during the past fortnight for two reasons:
The visit to Thailand of Premier Tojo and the occasion of Pibul's 46th
birthday which occurred on July 14.
THAI POLITICS
The Tojo Visit: Japan's Premier arrived in Bangkok on July 3 where
he held conferences with military officials, received decorations from
the Thai king, attended a state banquet and conferred on both days of
his visit with Premier Songgram. Public announcement was. made of only
one of the topics discussed at these conferences--the transfer to
Thailand of four Malay and two Shan'states. However, it was reported
that "complete accord" was reached on the "common objectives of the two
nations" (Tokyo in Japanese to the south seas, July 5).
Thailand has thus been enlarged by the acquisition of the Malay
states of P rlis, Kedah, Kelantan and Trenganun and the Shan states of
Kengtung and Mongpan. Justification of the transfer was based upon the
historial fact that all these areas had previously at one time or another,
been included in the territory of Thailand. Political implications
of the deal are omitted in broadcast references. It is reported to be
an effort on the part of Japan to correct an injustice and to repey
Thailand for the cooperntion extended during the drive on Singepore end
after. "This arrangement was made solely out of respect for the sovereiagity
and independence of Thailand and not on the basis of the so-celled 'give
and take' or mechinations as usually employed by the United States and
Britain" says Tokyo in a broadcast to the Americas on July 6.
These claims of historical justification are substantiated by Tengku
Nara Wanga, member of the Trengannu state council, whoseid that Trengannu
"always looked to Thailand as overlord since 1776, but as a result of
Anglo-Thai treaty of 190l, came under British rule. He related that be-
fore the British ceme, a periodical exchange of gifts was carriod out
between Thailand and Trongannu which was a token of good will between
the people" (Tokyo, English Domei, July 10).

AUSTRALIA
Receiving only slight attention in Melbourne broadcasts of this past fort-
night, the lection campaign is overshadowed by the perennial issue of
federal-state relations revived last week at the State Premiers' Conference.
Australian war expenditures and an expanded shipbuilding program are
reported.
STATE PREMIERS ACCEPT FEDERAL COUNCIL
State Premiers and Federal Cabinet Ministers' meeting in Melbourne on
July 13 adopted a plan for a National Works Council. According to Mr.
Curtin, the Council will probably have a permanent status in peacetime,
and will then have executive authority for the general public works program
of the whole nation. For the duration of the war, however, it will limit
its activities to planning a comprehensive program of post-war reconstruc-
tion projects. The decision to form such a Council marks an important
departure from the existing policy in Australia in regard to public works;
they have in the past been directed by seven governments and many munici-
palities rather than one over-all agency.
Explaining to the Conference that the Commonwealth Ministry for Post-
War Reconstruction had already been established to assure smooth transition
without delay from war activity to peace-time programs and living standards,
the Prime Minister also pointed out that the planning at present was pre-
paratory and was intended to provide a sound foundation for post-war
activity. The successful completion of this task, he said, would call for
the use of Commonwealth and State administration. Treasurer Chifley, who
is Commonwealth Minister for Post-War Reconstruction, also emphasized the
necessity for a single authority to see that future undertakings were
properly related to one another. Other problems involved will be re-employ-
ment of men in peacetime occupations after demobilization from their present
jobs, decentralization plans, rural developments, housing, migration, and
overseas trade.
The National Works Council, which will be establfshed immediately, will
consist of representatives of the seven state governments in addition to
Mr. Curtin, who will serve as Chairman, and will meet late this year follow-
ing the federal election.
Also discussed at this Conference was a proposal by the Commonwealth
government that federal financial grants to the states should be reduced
or abolished where states were piling up surpluse as a result of the in-
creased volume of money in circulation arising out of war expenditures.
CAMPAIGN NOTES
The Case of the Missing Document: Inquiring into a statement made by
Labor Minister Warde that the Opposition had stolen a document from the
official defense files (see Radio Report on the Far East, No, 24, July 6,
1943, p. J-1), the Royal Commission of Investigation reported to Prime

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
K2-
Minister Curtin that the files were complete and no dacument had been or
was missing. Following the release of this report, Mr. Curtin said that
since the rights and privilcges of Parliament were involved, Mr. Warde
would continue to abstain from the administration of his department. The
whole question will be submitted to the new Parliament when it meets after
the Federal election on August 21.
Political Information for Soldiers: Mr. Forde, Minister for the Army,
and Australian Army Chiefs are discussing a plan under which it will be
possible to place election issues before Australian soldiers without lift-
ing the Army ban on political. speeches in camps. It is expected that
arrangement will be made for publishing in Army journals the policy speeches
of each party leader with names, parties, and other necessary descriptions
of candidates.
WAR ECONOMY
Expenditures Summarized: During the past four years, Australia has
spent the equ1valent of $3,600,000,000 on the war. Of this $1,822,000,000
has been spent on war exponditures for the past year, according to a
report recently released by Treasurer Chifley. Civil expenditures for
the past year amount to only $351,O00,000. Income tax collections in-
creased from about $141,000,000 in 1941 to about $462,000,000 in the last
financial year. The actual returns were about $24,500,000 above these
estimated by the Treasurer.
Shibuilding Expansion: Undaunted by Berlin's report that Australian
shipyards and dry docks are so overtaxed with repairing ships that new con-
struction are entirely out of.the question (Transocean, July 6), the
Australian War Cabinet announces on July 10 that an expanded shipbuilding
program will be undertaken. Appropriations for building and repairs have
been increased by more than 33 1/3% on the advice of a special committee.
An important feature of the new plan is a provision for a fleet of 30 new
ships ranging from 10,000 to 20,000 tons for the Australian coastal and
Pacific island trade. Navy Minister Makin emphasized that naval and
merchant marine repairs will continue to have higher priority than new
construction, adding, however, that "The Australian shipbuilding industry
has come to stay. The foundations are being laid for an industry essential
to the future of an enormous maritime country" (Mclbourne in English to
Western North America).

II.
JAPANESE ACTIVITIES IN LATIN
AMERICA
Japan's interest in Latin America began shortly
after the first World War, and became more insistent
after Hitler's ascent to power. Particularly since
the outbreak of the present var in Europe, Japan in-
creased its effort toward closer relations with Latin
America. Innumerable commercial, scientific and
cultural missions arrived in almost all these countries,
headed by suave and polished Japanese gentlenen who
spoke Spanish or Portuguese fluently. Some missions
came over to visit and see the country for the promotion
of Japanese tourist travel. They negotiated trade
agr ements, mostly for th purchase of strategic materinll
for which they offered, frequently on a barter basis,
consumer goods manufactured under the extremely low
wage conditions of Japanese labor, at prices below those
of American or European products.
Cultural and Racial Propaganda
It must be admitted that in their "cultural" propa-.
ganda the Japanese shor considerable subtlety, certainly
more than the Germans or ven the Anglo-Saxons. They
seem generally quite content in publishing purely
abstract cultural articles without any special refer-
ence .to imnediate sims. They supply to magazines

The Japanese in Mexico
Mexico not only broke relations, but has actually
been at war with Japan since May 1942. No longer is
the Japanese embassy in Mexico in a position to listen,
see and report on joint United States-Mexico defense
activity along the West coast of Mexico. Despite
the relatively small number of Japanese and the un-
known strength of the pro-Axis elements among the
Mexican Sinarquistas who are probably inspired by the
Spanish Falangists, the Mexican government took,
since Pearl Harbor, energetic measures to hamper all
Japanese attempts working through Mexican fascist
groups to isolate Mexico from the United States.
Japan's long range colonization and investment
program along Mexico's Pacific coast, a program in
which military considerations were paramount, has
been completely upset.
For many years, Japanese government agencies,
development and colonization companies have been
interested in agriculture, water power, business
opportunity, immigration and colonization along the
West coast of Mexico. On the Pacific, Mexico has

The Japanese in Peru
In the bestern bulge of South America, in Peru,
which was the first to establish diplomatic relations
with Japan (1873), its subjects began to arrive.
shortly before the end of the twentieth century. At
that time they unaoubtedly came for the sole purpose
of earning a living, without any ulterior political
motivcs. They first morked on cotton and sugar plant-
ations but after some time started to drift into
towns vhere they first became domestic servants and
later established snall businesses such as barber and
beauty shops, groceries, coffee houses, refroshment
stands, bakcries, flomer or candy stores end toy
shops. Gradually they almost monopolized these lines
in most towns and finally they began operating large
stores in the capital and most of the electrical
repair and machine shops.
After the end of the firet world War the Japan-
ese Government began to airect and control the emigre-
tion of its subjects. This was followed by system-
atic utilization of Nipponese settlers in. Peru in
the interest of the economy and the foreign aspirations
of Japan.

IV
JAPAN'S BLUEPRINT OF ITS "NEW ORDER"
The "heaven on earth" vhich the Japanese promise
the rest of the vorld after uniting it "under one
roof" is. not difficult to visualize. We have only
to look at Japan itself and see how the Japanese
people live and how they are governed, what is
their standard of living and their government, which
is so willing to bestom its protection on the rest
of the world. Most European and American observers
who lived in Japan any length of time, and looked
below the surface of the exaggerated but meaning
less politeness of the upper classes, but came
in contact with the masses of the people, know
that the real Japan is far from being the land
of eternal cherry blossoms, gayety and. sweetness
as represented in prints and operettas. It is
a country of eternally half starved peasants, of
hungry children working long hours, of workers
without rights to combine in trade unions or to
form political parties to improve their medieval
standard of living; of women dragging coal in the
mines; of sweated comestic industries with women
and children working fourteen or fifteen hours a

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.99)

where medieval tortures are used to extract

The Workers:_
In 1938 the "Standard of Living Report" of
the Internat ional Labor Office of the League of
Nations, reported that twenty five percent of
Japanese families, and thirty seven percent of
Japanese children were not earning enough to main-
tain health and efficiency. Japanese law permits
children to work beginning at the age of twelve.
In 1935 a survey of the larger factories of the
nation showed that over sixteen percent of the
workers were unaer sixteen. On top of this a
leading Japanese econonist, Seichi Kojima, estim-
ated in January 1941, that the standard of living
had declined forty percent since the outbreak of the
Sino-Japanese war in July 1937. *)
This did not deter a Japanese general from
admonishing his people that in view of the war:
"The lomering of the people's
living standards and increased savings
are of vital importan ce. Taking the
people of Japan as a whole, each one
must live on one-third of his monthly

The Peasants:
An investigation carried out in 1937 by the
Japanese Linistry of Agriculture showed that most
of Japan's farm families border on destitution.
In that year three-quarters of the 5,575,000 fam
households owned less than one hectare each. (The
average size of a farm in the United States is
sixty three hectarcs.) Seventy percent of the
farmers rented all or part of th ir l ne from tho
landowners, who exacted rent calculated at seven
times that paid in England. Rice rents were usually
paid in kind; on an average the farmer hands over
to the landlord more than half of his crop. *)
The lot of the peasant-owners is little better.

In an address made on January 16, 1943 to the
Woman's National Republican Club in New York, Mr.
Joseph C. Grew, who for many years was United States
ambassador to Japan, resumed the situation and the
attitude we nust take for our own security and
future:
"What worries me in the attitude of our
fellow country-men is.......the utterly fall-
acious pre-war thinking which still widely
persists, that the Japanese.....are incapable
of developing such power as could ever serfously
threaten our home shores, our cities and our
homes, a habit of mind which is reinforced by
the great distances separating our homeland
from the Far. Eastern and Southern Pacific battle
fronts today.
"Moral stimulation is good but moral
complacency is the most dangerous habit of mind
we can develop, and that danger is serious and
ever-present. I have seen with my own eyes in
some cases and I have had first hand vivid per-

your review, use and comment a rather comprehensive
study entitled "Japan's Menace to the Americas."
894.20210/227
This is of timely interest in view of the probability
that operations in the Pacific theater of the war will
be intensified during the coming months.
This study was prepared by Mr. Stephen Naft, inter-
national journalist and scholar, who also prepared
the roport "One Hundred Years of German Ambitions in
Latin America" which was so widely used. In both
studies Miss Gabrielle Swados advised Mr. Naft on
the suitability of the material for radio use. Kr.
Naft consulted the most significant publications in
this country on the subjects of Japanese ambitions
for world rule, Japanese activities in Latin America,
and Mikado worship and the "New Order." The work was
done under the general supervision of Mr. Russell
Pierce.

Chapter III
m1s chapter disousses the mythe of Japan's origin
bries in the Book of Genesis constitute the Christian
story of the beginnings of man. In general the state-
ments appearing in this chapter are true and have been
made by Japanese leaders from time to time. I believe
that it 1s a mistake to state that the Japanese propose
to impose their own religion on other nations. They have
1mposed it on the Koreane, but these people have the
status of Japanese subjeots. The recent trend has been
for the Japanese to attempt to divert or utilize the
religions in ocoupied territories for their own use. In
this oonneotion, it 1s suggested that for propaganda pur-
poses in South America it might be well to utilize the
manner in whioh the Japanese Army in the Philippines 1s
using the Catholio Churoh there to further its own
political ends.
Chapter IV
This chapter which attempts to desoribe living and
social conditions 1: Japan 1s, it 1s thought, distinotly
unfair. It is true that the people are worked very hard,
that the agrioultural situation is bad, that there 1s
widespread poverty, and that women do not enjoy as favor-
able a position in soclety as in western nations. However,
these people are used to these conditions and know no other;
henoe it cannot be said that they are unhappy or are grumbling
against conditions. The desoription on page 74 of the position
of Japanese women in society is inacourate. At the present
time women have certain legal rights, and, for inatanee,
cannot be divoreed without cause as was formerly the case.
Neither can a husband sell his wife to a feotory or 
brothel without her consent, although in certain oiroum-
stanoes a father may do so. On pages75 and 76 there 1s a
deseription or a white slave "monopoly", extraeted from a
sensational book by Alexander Pernikeff. It is true that
the abuses desoribed in his book aetually teok plaee, but
It would be Just as true to state that Al Oapone was typseel
of every Amerioan city.
Chapters V and vI
I have no partioular suggestions in regard to shese
chapters. It 1s assumed that this material will met be

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.99)
Honorable Adolf A. Berle, Jr.
The fifth communication which has been intercepted in this case is
one postmarked at Buenos Aires on February 10, 1943, addressed to Candido
Santidrian (Castillo). The encoded message enolosed in this communication has
been decoded and the plain text has been set out starting on page 18 of the
memorandum previously submitted to you. Investigation in Santiago, Chile, fails
to reveal the identity of the addressee. It has been ascertained, however,
that Alberto Veloz (Santa Cruz), a Chilean and known Japanese propagandist, is
the owner of Casilla 6lOl, Correo No. 5, Santiago, Chile, which is the address
of the communication to Santidrian. He has acted as an intermediary for the
Japanese in the case entitled "Tomigyo Koseki," information concerning which has
been previously furnished you, and it is apparent that he has furnished the
Japanese involved in this espionage ring with the facilities for receiving
communications from Buenos Aires. It is also apparent that he has furnished
the cover name of Candido Santidrian (Castillo) which is quite similar to the
name of his father-in-law, Candido Santidrian (Teran). Investigation reveals
that the real Candido Santidrian (Teran) is not engaged in espionage activity,
and it is thought that his name has probably been used without his consent.
Santidrian is the holder of Chilean Prontuario Civil No. 1,46l,437, which reveals
that he was born February 2, 1880 in Burgos, Spain; that he still maintains his
Spanish citizenship; that he arrived in Chile in 1907; and that he presently
resides at Calle Cuevas l494. He is a man of humble origin and is practically
illiterate; it is believed that he is in no way engaged in subversive activity.
All the Japanese subjects who have resided in Chile and who have
been active in instant espionage case have been repatriated to Japan. The re-
patriation took place in September of 1943 and the following mentioned individuals
have left Chile for Japan:
Takeo Ozasa
Takeo Takano
Kennosuke Kawaguchi
Kaname Nakayama
Teruo Miyaki
Fukunosuke Nomura
Yei Mamiya
Akira Abe
Sueuki Nakatsuka
Ko Kawamura
Itsuki Takada
Motozo Hattori
Yonosuke Hagiwara
Akira Uye
It will be remembered that Goro Miyasaki, the former manager of the
Compania Chilena Oriental, is known to have arranged for and planned the robbery
against the American Cultural Attache in Santiago, Chile, in the belief that he
was the intelligence officer of the American Embassy. It will also be remembered
that at the time that Miyasaki's location was searched at Arica, Chile, by agents
of the Department of Investigations a number of maps of ports, airports, coastal
defenses and mineral regions were found. From July until September 12, 1945,
Miyasaki lived at 165 Avenida Olmos, City of Cordoba, Argentina, with Hidenosuke
Ueda under technical detention, awaiting deportation as an undesirable alien.
During this period both subjeots had apparently traveled throughout the Province
of Cordoba without restriotion and contacted both Japanese and German nationals
who are suspected of being engaged in espionage activities. On September 12,
Miyasaki left Cordoba and journeyed to Buenos Aires, and on October 10, l94s,
Ueda followed him to that city. Both are presently living in the Alvear palace
Hotel.

advised, however, that both communications were written by the same
person. These comnunications were addressed respectively to DR.
ALBERTO GOES TELLES, Avenida Rio Branco 143, 4th floor, Rio de Janeiro
and to DR. ARY P. de ANDRADE FIGUEIRA, Rua do Rosario 107. 3rd floor,
Rio de Janeiro.

TRANSLATOR'S NOTE:
(x) The Portuguese word "Carimbo" signifies an "impression
seal", "rubber stamp" or some similar "stamp" for placing
a mark or impression on somothing. It does not mean
"postage stamp". The only "stamp" or "scal" of this sort
appoaring on this letter is tho "cancollation mark" on the
envelope.
(xx) The Portugueso word usod appears to bo "sinete" meaning
"seal".
Source "A" has advised that a photographic copy of the envelope
and the letter was checked against the files of Source "A" without
effecting an identification. Source "A" pointed out, however, that
the writer of the letter in the first paragraph may have been trying
to inform NAGAI of the whereabouts of SHIMEKITI, TAMURA, NAKAJIMA and
IzUmI. It was suggested that these may be actual names or code names.
It appears that the places referred to, namely, Marilia, District of
Paulista-Noroeste and Mogiare sections of São Paulo. It was pointed
out that the sentence "For tho time being nothing is known concerning
their return to their homes." may be a doublo meaning for "For the
time being not hing is known concerning their roturn to Japan". In-
asmuch as the list of Brazilian ropatriatos was at that time being
made up, the writor of the lettor may have beon roferring to indi-
viduals who would embark on tho SS Gripsholm at Rio do Janeiro.
Source "A" also pointed out that the reference in the second
paragraph to the stamp seems to be suspicious. It was not possible
to examine the postage stamp and seal on this letter, inasmuch as
it was impossible under the circumstances to retain the letter for any
length of time. Source "A" suggested that this paragraph may have been
- 22-

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.98)
designed to attract the addressous attcntion to tho stamp which by
previous agroomont, might roprosont a code indicator.
Also in tho socond paragraph, tho writer stated "I remombered to
writo today in commcmoration of the contonary of tho first Brazilian
t ip. Thus, obsorve on tho canccllation stamp of this lotter a
cial scal." It was ascortainod that the stamp on tho lettor was
ctually an ordinary ono and not of the commomorativo issuo. It was
determinod, howcver, that a now stemp was at that timo boing printod
in Brazil in commomoration of tho lo0th Annivursary of tho First
Postago Stamp in Brazil which was known as "OLHo DE BOI". It was
notod by Sourco "A" that the writor of tho lotter in tho second
paragraph spocifically roforrcd to the canccllation stamp and not to
the postage stamp. It ppoars that a cancollation stamp may havo
actually bocn a spocial soal.
Source "A" also obsorved that the rofurunce in tho third para-
graph "The brother who was resting" may refer to someone who was
under surveillance. If the paragraph is interpreted as follows:
"Thanks to the Lord we are ail well, the brother who is resting

hensive) . He undoubtedly needs some time to strengthen himself."

Bureau concerning the operation of the Southern Cotton Company and others

1942 and was subsequently repatriated to Japan having left Jersey City,
New Jersey on September 2, 1943 aboard the "SS Gripsholm."

RE: "JAPSA CASE"
On December 8, 1 41, the Peruvian Government froze all
Japanese funds in Peru. However, it was not until Jantary 24, 1942
at the conference in Rio de Janeiro that the Peruvian dvernment
saw fit to freeze the funds of the remainder of the Axis Wationals.
Law No.9577, passed Larch :, 1942, gave to the President er Peru
the supreme power to expropriate all businesses and properties of
Axis Nationals. This law was general in nature and was superceded
by Law No.9586 on April 10, 1942. lHowever, inammuoh as this
latter law was not published until April 13, 1942, the Axis Matiomals
had three days to arrange the transfer of funds and the withareval
of sums of money so as to alleviate total or material loss of their
interests. This law provided for just appraisal, type of sale,
wanner of advertising, and also stated that titlo to instant
businesses or properties could pass only to native born Peruvians.
The Superintendency of Economy was named by the Govermmen
to handle the liquidation of xis interests. Thus it is interes
to note that the firms disoussed in this report have been liquie
by the Peruvian Government in accordance with the Expropriatéon L
It is also interesti. to note that the Japanese Espione
system in Peru was severely struined by the repatriation and
deportation policies adopted by the Feruvian Government in
cooperation with the United States, whereby hundreds of Japanese
have departed from Peru via United States Army Transports.

Information has been recelved which indicates the
"JAPSA" Espionage Ring has been operating in South America
principally through the Japanese Embassy at Santiago, Chile.
It has been reported that this Fspionage Ring flourished openly
until the break of relations in Chile 1n January, 1942. 1t
has been reported that the Compania Chilena Oriental, operating
in Santiago, Chile, gathered espionsge and commerclal information
throughout South America for the Japanese Government. It is
known that the Compania Chilena Oriental communicated with the
Japan-Colombia Trading Company of Bogotá, Colombia.
Reliable Source A reported that the Japan-Colombia

Trading Company, as such, was not registered under this name

MEMORANDU!
September 29, 1941
TO:
Mr. Lavrence Duggan
State Denartment
FROF:
Edward !. Robbins
SUJEUT: Japanese Activities and Propa anda
Yr. Harry Frantz, of our Information Division, is anxious
to make a rather complete study of Japanese activities and propaganda
throuvhout Latin America.
I should be exceedinyly grateful if the State Denartment could
make available to our field office, through whom all such material is
gatheced for the rest of the Coordinator's Office, material on this
question. And I vonder whether it would be possible for this materi al
to answer the following questions:
(1) The location, numhers, and economic activities of
the important Japanese colonies in the other American Republics.
(2) A general outline of the past political and related
activities of the Japanese in the other American Republics, naturally
with special emphasis on such of these as may have impinged on U. S.
interests or policy.
(3) An analysis of the policy of the Japanese Government
itself regarding these Japanese colonies, with emphasis on specific
Japanese activities carried on at the official instance of the Tokyo
authorities themselves.
(4) An analysis of political and other inter-relationships
between the Japanese colonies in the other American Republics and the
Germans and Italians there.
(5) The opinion of students of the problem of the Japanese
in the other American tepublics, regarding the trend of such Japanese
activities and what problems or difficulties are anticipated in this
connection.

We have refrained from making  study of this particular
problem ourselves, in the beliof t at tho State Department has
unquestionably covered the subject most thoroughly and that duplica-
tion is altogether undesirable. Ye are most anxious to make this
information available to the division of the Coordinator's Office
which has requested it, and, as I say, we shall greatly aporeciate
your assistance in doing so.

19/73
Oleor. May 3. 1972
Summary of Information:
NARS Dato.
RY
8-15
ITEM:
Noburo HARA and Cia., Japanese textile firm in Buenos Aires,
Argentina is a center for the collection of British, U.S., neutral
and also Argentine maritime infornation for the japanese. It is also
apparently the destination of maritime intelligence obtained on
the West Coast. Dr. Albino PUGNALIN, former Legal Advisor to the
Japanese Embassy and alleged head of a martime espionage ring;
Tomiya KOSEkI, former Embassy secretary and known agent; Olga ALEssAnDRI,
espionage intermediary; Adm. Katsumi YUKISHITA, ex-Japanese Naval Attache
and alleged chief of Japanese maritime intelligence, have all either
called at or been in contact with HARA & Cia. The firm has avoided
blacklisting through the ruse of transferring ownership to two Argentine
employees, Cleto SANCHEZ and Jaime GARCIA, and is now known as SANCHEZ
and GARCIA. Both of these individuals are reported to be intermediaries
in the Japanese espionage ring.
SoURCE: M/A, Argentina, Report #11839, 27 October 1944; Evaluation B-3.
COMMENT:
The Argentine Government severed relations with the Axis in January
1944. Since that time there has been little control placed over former
members of the Japanese Embassy staff, or over citizens or aliens of
Japanese origin. Ineffective steps, largely window dressing, were taken
in February to curtail the activities of Japanese espionage agents.
Since that time such individuals have been allowed to operate with no
interference from the Argentine Government. The above report is but
additional confirmation of the existance of an active maritime collection
agency forwarding data to the Japanese.
P.D.Q!

=== Page 535 === (confidence 0.95)
ALERT INCIDLNT
It is reported that after the Tuna boat "Alert" reached
Puntarens, Costa ica, with a Japanese crew on hoard, it left Puntarenas
to go to Caldera to secure lo,Ooo gallons of oil, with the Japanese
crew still on board.
Besides the crew two sons of Rafael Beache (Pafael Beeche,Jr.
and Chanin Beche) and a Customs guard from Puntarenas were on board.
The West India Cil Tanks are locate at Caldera and the Customs guard,
one Jose Ma Castro, had an order for oil for the Customs guard, Mr.
Faniagua at Caldera.
When the "Alert" reached Culdera it was found that 5,000
gallons of oil remained in the tanks of the boat and also the hose used
for transferring the oil from the tanks to the boat was broken. The
boat started back to untarenas and shortly thereafter the attack by
United States planes began.
The above information is in variance with previous reports which
state that the attack took lace before the "Alert" reached Caldera.
Rafael Beeche, the Port Captain at Puntarenas, recently stated
that he had received a letter from a United States Military man in
Washington, D. C., asking him to relay the facts of the matter in his
official capacity as Fort Captain. However, Beeche stated that he owned
some stock in the Company that owned the "Alert" and that it was almost
certain that if he wrote out the facts and signed it, the United States
Government would undoubtedly confiscate the "Alert." He stated that he
therefore tore up the letter from Washington and is not sure from whom
the letter was received but believes the Military man's name was Steever
or Steiner.
It is reported that Rafael Beeche did not remove the Japanese
crew from the "Alert" when it reached Puntarenas and before it went to
Caldera because he is the agent for the West India Oil Company and wanted
to make a sale of oil which would not have been possible had the Japanese
crew been removed at that time.
INR
AU6 5-1942
7me
41
